PrEASANT PARAGRAPIES.

TrrE Crust of Society—Macaroons. MANY a cook can spoil the broth without any

assistance whatever. - THERE is nothing more disagreeable than being told things for your own good. THE trouble with most poets is that they are made, not born. " DunEs will smoke the deadly cigarette." " Yes ; that's one good thing about dudes." MODESTY consists in refraining from expressing your opinion of yourself. DON'T place too much confidence in yourself— there is no one who more frequently deceives you. "You know I only live to make you happy," murmured the young man. "Dear me !" she said, "you oughtn't to go to all that trouble." "I MAY be young," said the very young man, "but my l4ere for your daughter is as strong and true as if I were whitened by the snows of in- numerable winters." LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST NELLIE,

There are many changes and rumours of change in dresses this year. It is quite possible that the close-fitting sleeve may be re-introduced, which will seem like a revolution. About bodices, most women, I may tell you, are still wearing them becomingly full and round-waisted. The reason is that the plain, close-fitting bodice demands a lovely figure, and one rigorously cor- seted too, and as the reign 6f the blouse has done away with tight-lacing for the time being, even the most ardent devotees of fashion are loth to revert to the "girdle of eighteen inches" again. But although the blouse shape is retained more or less, the blouse proper is practically a thing of the past, as far as well-dressed women are concerned. A stately evening dress is of pale green satin, the back of the skirt set in huge round folds that form a demi-train ; the sides of the skirt fell quite plain and rather close to the figure, and had broad panels of cheno brocade in vivid old-fashioned pink and green design on a white ground, studded with crystals. A single row of crystals was set at each edge. A flat panel of white Brussels lace, attached with pearl and silver gimp, was set straight down the front, and had an exquisitely delicate effect on the satin. The bodice was fashioned in a long well-shaped point front and back, the side-pieces being of the cheno brocade, brought down with a graceful curve to the point, while there was a vest of pearl and silver, worked with white satin, and a deep fall of white lace round the square bodice, and falling over the huge butterfly-shaped sleeves of green satin and chene. Venetian cloth is one of the prettiest materials that are being worn just now. A very pretty costume is made in this

material, the colour being light blue. The bodice, which is awfully smart, has a tabbed collar, fronts, and cuffs of white cloth or satin braided with a fine cord. _ .

----- Some of the newest black velvet coats, hanging in loose, straight pleats from neck to finish, with large collars of chinchilla, mink, Persian lamb, or caracal, large ornamental buttons, are belted in to the figure at the back. They are eminently becoming to women of slight and tall proportions, but should be religiously eschewed by the short, and those of pronounced contours. A pretty little loose coat of sand-coloured cloth has a deep collar and lapeLs of mink, and a mink removable waist- coat, for which, when temperature permits, is sub- stituted an elaborate jabot of ivory lace.

- - - - A very stylish double-breasted loose fronted driv- ing coat is made of dark blue beaver cloth, the seams widely strapped, and a handsome felt collar

and cuffs of sable tail. The coat is lined through- out with a large blue and gold plaid silk. I don't think I have said anything lately about head-gear for ladies. If you followed my hint of some weeks ago, however, you will at least be in the swim. I mean what I said about beefeater hats, whose popularity is increasing every day. They really deserve to be popular, for they are as a rule extremely handsome. This depends to some extent upon the trimming. For this purpose ospreys look better than anything. Toques, too, maintain the favour with which they have been for some time regarded. Tout le monde wears them, and they are of all materials and colours. Of course, some kind of fur or velvet is the right material. The colour is a matter of taste, but if yours is like mine you will fight shy of the very light-coloured toques one sees worn. Some dark kind of velvet is as good as anything. Felt hats are not being worn quite so much, but they are still stylish. Black is more fashionable than grey or light colours. A smart

chapeau of this kind has a draped crown of moss green mirror velvet, a pretty bow of shot ribbon, nearly at the back, moss green quills with black and white osprey, one side fastened with paste batton, and paste button in front. Your loving

Vs OMEN'zi A As Lent approachss, ve iding.s increase. During the present week I have been asked to inspect no less than seven trousseaux, and although not of the sumptuous- ness of a Vanderbilt, they each contained frocks, capes, and frou-frous that were very smart and fascinating,

Plush is in full fashion again and among tlv many trea- sures of one particular trousseaux I espied a lovely white plush tea-gown lined with pale pink silk, and with a front of deep coffee-colored lace, over white silk. This lovely garment is destined to be worn by one of the most fascinating brunettes in England. Numbers of the mid- season capes and coats are in black, brown and dark blue plush and there is to be a decided revival of this favourite material again. Bradford looms are busy with orders for it, so that there is no doubt about the fact. The aatelaine with its pendant frivolities has been revived, and fashionable women remind us just now of the lady in the nursery rhyme who "is to have music wherever she goes." With regard to the utility of the appendages, they are pretty, and when we have said this, we have said all that is possible. Who among us, for example ever saw a pair of chatelaine scissors that would out? Is the chatelaine thimble ever to be found in its velvet-lined receptacle? Has the chatelaine vin- aigrette ever any vinegar in it? Does the chatelaine fan- holder ever miss a chance of losing the fan entrusted to its charge? Is:the chatalaine bouquet-holder more worthy? Certainly not! Their small silvery clink seems to be always saying "Look at us ! Look at us! We are so pretty that you cannot expect us to be useful."

To be, or not to be, that is the question, as some famous person once observed in reference to some other less important matter—is it to be plain skirts, or is it to be trimmed skifts ? There is no doubt that we are decidedly threatened with the full liberally trimmed skirts whose penderous attractions we have resisted " womanfully " for two seasons now and those of us who cling to the joy of a perfect simplicity will note with sorrow the tact that much be-trimmed skirts are likely to be correct during the coming summer. But to return to my subject, viz., weddings.according to present arrangements there will be ne fewer than three royal marriages during the next six months; all the brides being related to our Royal family of England. The marriage of the Princess Louise of Denmark, the neice of the Princess of Wales, is to take place in March. Then comes the youthful Princess Alexandra of Saxe- Coburg-Gotha. whose marriage with the Hereditary Prince of Hohenlohe-Langenberg is fixed for April, and lastly comes Princess Mend of Wales who is to be married in the spring or early summer to her cousin Prince Carl of Denmark. There is no truth in the report that the marriage of Princess Maud is to oe postponed. Of course there will be a curtailment of pageant, but this will ex- actly fit in with the wishes of both bride and bride- groom. The Princess is desirous that her marriage should be solemnised at Sandringham, but in this, as in all similar cases the Queen is paramount, and it is as- serted on good authority that Her Majesty would prefer St. George's, Windsor, or the private chapel in Bucking- ham Palace.

- --- Rumours are afloat to the effect that the Govenorship

of the Isle of Wight will offered to Princess Henry of Battenberg. If the Princess should accept the honour it will not be the first time in the history of the Island that the government has been given to a lady. Between 1283 and 1293, Isabella de Fortibus reigned over ths Isle of Wight as a feudal chief after which she ceded her sovereignty of the Island, to the Crown for about 260,000.

It appears the tomb of Prince Henry of Battenberg in the Whmpingham Church is eventually to be covered by a recumbent statue of the Prince, somewhat similar to that of the Prince Consort in the Albert Memorial Chapel at Windsor, and a window in the church is to be filled with appropriate stained glass. The work on the cenotaph,if not executed by Princess Louise,Marchioness of Lorne, will probably be entrusted either to Mr. Brock or Mr. Alfred Gilbert, both of whom are Royal Academi- cians. Princess Louise studied modelling for several years with the late Sir Edgar Joseph Boehm, and has producod some really excellent work. Her instructor was probably the cleverest English sculptor of his day. He was English by adoption only, having teen born in Vienna of Hungarian parents. He came to London in search ef fortune, and the patronage he received from Royalty ensured him plenty of commissions elsewhere. It is indeed one of the ironies of fate that England should,in a single week,be deprived of the two men who represent English art and music, for while Lord Leigh- ton indisputably held that position as painter there is no one who, like Sir Joseph Barnby, so completely justifies the title of representative English musician. A York- shireman, born and bred, his peculiarly English person- ality had an enormous attraction for that great section of the public which makes rather for the solid than the subtle in music. He came, and worked, and died, in no sense as an originator, but in every sense as a developer of that musical side Of his nation in which he stands without a compeer. The amount and the quality of his actual composition are alike inconsiderable, they exhibit a certain luscious sweetness which is. distinctly his weakness as a musician,but where large academic inter- pretations of the sacred masterpieces of Handel, Men- delssohn, and Spohr,were concerned,he stood unrivalled. Rumour whispers that Miss Barnby will presently train for the vocal profession,while one at least of her brothers will adopt the black and white art as a calling. When the time comes for her debut the only daughter of Sir Joeeph Barnby will certainly not fail to arouse public in- terest, for she possesses a beautiful voice. Mr. Charles Bantley, whose son Michael is worthily maintaining the family tradition at the Empire Theatre in London, will shortly entor upon his sixty-third year. The veteran vocalist,who inherits his musical talent from his father, formerly a well-known organist at Liverpool. made his first appearance in his present profession as a tenor in the choir at the opening of the Philharmonic Hall at Liverpool in 1849, but subsequently became chief bass at a neighbouring Catholic Church. For two years after his majority our foremost baritone studied his art at Milan under Gaetano Nava, and in London under Manuel Garcia. making his first great success at the Leeds Musical Festival in 185 S. Mr. Bantlev married in 1859 a grand-daughter of the famous tragedian Charles Kemble.

A few days ago I came across an amusing article written by a young American woman, on the " Art of Keeping Young-looking." She maintains that French- women understand this delicate art best, next come Americans and then Englishwomen—all other nationali- ties apparently are out of count. Excitement, mental activity, an interest in life, and a carelessness of tem- perament, she goes on to say, are essential to the art of keening young,and in support of her theory she mentions the undoebtedly curious fact, that actresses keep their youth and good looks longer than any other class of women. lam extremely doubtful of this particular assertion. In the first instance it is rather difficult to know what actresses really look like au naturel, and probably most middle-aged women could renew the appearance of youth did they choose to do so by the aid of a large amount of art and small amount of daylight. There is, howener, no doubt but that women who lead listless, stagnant lives age much more rapidly than those whose lives are kept in reasonable activity, even if this activity frequently be accompanied by late hours. But is it not possible that one might sacrifice too much to this fancy for looking young? And is the case of the lady, cited by the writer of the article, who at sixty-five years of age captivated the other sex by her youthful bloom, so very admirable and worthy of imitation ? Mi.:ldle-age has its own dignity and attractions, and the desire for conquest at this period hardly perhaps increases either. When mentioning this small article to a woman promi- nent in the literary world, and whose complexion is equal to a young girl's, she assured me that of all the man-7 precautions she has 'aken to keep her skin in good conditinn. none was so efficacious as hot milk. " When I am frinhtfully fatigued," she said. "from the rush of the life I lead, I net a gallon of milk and put it in my bath, adding sufficient hot water to cover the body. Lie in this mixture for ten minutes and come out feeling thoroughly refreshed, and with a new life to the skin which previous to the bath had a dead look." Maim me Patti says that the reason she bas kept her youthful looks is, because she never leses her temper. "No woman she remarks can keep young looking.who often loses her temper when I feel my temper getting the better of me I always get up and leave the room." —FeENCii-Perar PUDDING. —Take one pound of French plums and boil them until the stones slip out easily Make a batter of half a pound of flour and three eggs, add a pinch of salt and stir in the plums, which must he well drained.. Boil in a well-buttered basin for two hours. . _

" MADGE."

Girls in North Branch Michigan, have organised's' Leap-year Association, the object of which is" to get all the members married off before the end of the rear.'lhe scheme of campaign is yet in process of formation. Sosnitza, in Upper Silesia, has a sleeping girl, who, in her waking intervals, divulges communieations which she professes to have received directly from the Virgin. Thousands of people flock to see the miracle, and the police authorities also have her under observation. A woman will he drafted for service in the French army next year, for the reason that at her birth she was erroneously registered as a male child. Though tha officials are cognisant of the blunder, red tape makes it essential for her to present herself for military duty. Mexican women are indefatigable dancers, and on a moonlight night it is a pleasant sight to see hundreds of white robed Mexicans dancing the fandango, which is the social or street dance of the common people. Mexi- can women will travel any distance for a dance, coming to these entertainments either on horseback or in carts that will frequently convey the whole family,and which are drawn by fleet-footed oxen. In Hungary women can studs, at all the universities and may enter all the " scientific " professions, but they may only take up three " practical" professions. They are allowed to teach in secondary girls' schools, to prac- tice medicine and surgery (upon women only, and to be dispensing chemists. The Hungarians look upon the new Act ',chief' makes these things possible as a piece of unessaren'ed Liberalism.

There are no undertakers in Japan. When a person dies in that country the nearest relatives purchasea box or coffin and bury the body. The period of mourning does not begin until after the burial.

Bicycle Proverbs.

One of the clever people has been at pains to invent a variety of proverbs for the benefit of bicycle-riders. Here are a few of them : Stolen bikes run fast. Well tyred—least tired. A stone goes before a fall. A ring in time saves crime. Too many bicycles spoil a walk. Spare the pump and spoil the tyre. It is no use sighing over spilt cycles. Where there's a way there's a wheel. Between two wheels you come to woe. A novice and her bike are soon parted. The world's a cycle, and all society merely cyclists. Look at others before you put on rational costume. Never run over a magistrate; the pleasure is not worth the cost.

One man can set a beginner on a bicycle, but ten can': keep him there. She that rideth uprightly rideth surely, but she that perverteth her way shall fall.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY FEBRUARY 15. 1896

REMARKABLE STATEMENT BY A HOSPITAL PA.TIENT.

"SURELY there must be some mistake," I exclaimed in my surprise, when, on inquiring for Miss Florence Vitler, I was presonted to a buxom, rosy-complexioned girl, looking the picture of health. But there was no error, and Miss Vitler, whose home is at Pleasant View cottages, Hooe, near Battle, said that not long ago she was too ill to keep any situation, and was compelled to remain at home.

"How did you get:into this weak condition 2"

"1 had a bad attack of influenza," said Miss Vitler, "and I think that brought it on. I had bad swollen legs. I could get about, but had to have them bandaged. This was about three years'ago, when I was sixteen years old. I got better of the swollen legs, but bad terrible fainting fits. I had pains in my sides, especially in my left side, and in my back. I used to come over very giddy, and my hands and feet became dreadfully cold, just before I had a fit. When I was sitting by a large fire my hands and feet would be quite cold. My heart used to cause much alarm, as it beat very fast. Everyone dround me became frightened. Indeed, I felt like dying. I had several different doctors, and was an out-patient at the Hastings, St. Leonards, and East Sussex Hospital for ' four months. I gavejup all hopes of getting better again I used to fall about in these fits, sometimes having three a day. I got into a very weak state, and any little thing would make me tremble."or send me into a fainting fit. I used to feel very tired when I had done scarcely any- thing : as tired when Igot up as when I went to bed." "Couldn't the doctors cure you ?"

" No ; though they were very skilful and able doctors. I had different doctors on and off, for about two years, besides attending the Hospital. This went on up to nine or ten montiv ago." -

_ "What cured you after all ? " I asked, " for you ap- pear to be in perfect health now."

"Oh yes, I could not wish to be better than I am now. It was Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People which cured me. My mother saw so many testimonials of people being cured by them that she got a box for me, The very first box seemed to do me good, and I have taken three boxes in all. I noticed a change in myself about a fortnight after I started taking the Pills. I seemed to feel so much better, and was not so tired. After that I got better and better, and did not seem like

the same person." Such is this remarkable history, afterwards vertified in every detail. and it confirms what is said la all the chief newspapers of this country of the astonishing cures wrought by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, which have had an unrivalled success as a tonic and strengthening medicine. They cure palpations, and the many disorders which follow iolluenzi or arise from an impoverished state of the blood,such as anternia,pale and sallow complexion, general muscular weakness, loss of appetite, shortness of breath, pains in the back.nervous headache, early decay, all forms of female weakness, hysteria, paralysis, locomotor ataxy, rheumatism ;nd sciatica. These Pills are genuine only with the full name Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and are sold by all chemists and by Dr. William' Medicine Company, 46, Holborn-viaduct, London, E.C., at 2s. 9d. a box, or- six for 13s. 9d. They invigorate the system after over work, worry, excesses and indiscretions of living.

THE EXTERMINATION OF TUE

A FRICA.N ELEPHANT.

In a letter recently addressed to the "Times," Mr. U. A. Bryden expresses a regret that, in the near future the African elephant will in all probability be extinct. This he lays to the blame of the ivory hunter, both black and white. In an interview he had with Khama chiet of Bamangwato—who country he visited in 1890—Mr. Bryden referred to this subject, and Kharna imparted to him the intelligence that in his wide dominions he ;had then but one herd of elephants left. The venerable chief of course remembers Gordon Cumming, and when that great bunter visited the Bamangwato territory the country was simply teeming with these huge beasts. After Dr. Livingstone had discovered Lake Ngami in 1849, and the indomitable white men began to press fir- ward, the number of the wild elephant began rapidly to diminish. In one year after the discovery of the lake, 900 ( lephant were shot in that district alone. "Tusks which before 1847 were in these regions looked upon by the natives as useless bones,"says Mr. Bryden, " quickly acquired a high value, and extermination followed quickly upon the heels of discovery." If the African elephant is to be preserved, there is not much time to be lost. The Germans in their parts of East and Central Africa have already taken steps to preserve this useful denizen of the forest, and it is to be hoped that their efforts will in every way be successful. Surely this is a parallel case to that of the North American buff4lo,which formerly roamed in thousands on the wide praries of the New Werld, and of which now there remain very few specimens, and these rarely to be seen.

SALES AND CURES PROVE THE MERIT OF HOOD'S SAR APARILLA.

The enormous sales of Hood's Sarsaparilla and its wonderful cures prove indisputably even more than has ever been claimed for the medicine, and give it a clear right to the title of the one true blood puri- fier prominently in the public eye to-day. We chal- lenge any medicine to show an appreciation at home like that which has been accorded Hood's Sarsa- parilla. Such results as have attended the use of flood's Sarsaparilla could come only from absolute merit, and the steadily increasing demand unmis- takably proves that the people appreciate Hood's Sarsaparilla. GIVES US STRENGTH.

WEAKENED SYSTEM GREATLY BENEFITED BY I HOOD'S SARSAPARILLA. "Gentlemen,—MY husband has taken Hood's Vegetable Pills, and finds they do him a lot of good, and he strongly recommends them to those suffering from headache and tired feeling. Seeing the names of the well-known herbs used in Hood's Sarsaparilla in one of your circulars, I was induced to try Hood's Sarsaparilla. and found it relieved the dreadful nervous pains I was suffering from.pro- needing from a diseased spine and nerves. After I had taken it a few days I gays it to my husband who was suffering from a ruptured spine bone. After he had taken it two days he began to feel much better, and we are still taking it. CAPITOLA THE MADCAP. BY MRS. E. D. E. N. SOUTIEWORTH.

CHAPTER XXIV. CAPITOLS MOTHER. " A woman like a dew-drop she was purer than the

purest, And her noble heart the noblest, yes, and her sure

faith the surest ; And her eyes were dark and humid like the depth in

- depth of lustre Hid i' the harebell, while her tresses, sunnier than

the wild grape's cluster, Gushed in raven-tinted plenty down her checks' rose- _ _ _

_ tinted marble : Then her voice's music—call it the well's bubbling, the birds' warble." _ .ROWNING. " CAP ? " "Sir 1" "What the blazes is the matter with you?" "What the blazes You better say what the dust and ashes! I'm bored to death 1 I'm blue as indigo 1 There never was such a rum old place as this, or such a rum old uncle as you I" "Cap ! how often have I told you to leave off this Bowery boy talk! Runs! bah 1" said 0 d

Hurricane. "Well, it is rum then! Nothing ever happens here 1 The silence deafens me! the plenty takes away my appetite! the safety makes me low 1 " "Hum 1 you are like the Bowery boys in times of peace, • spoiling for a fight.'" "Yes, I am ! just decomposing above ground for want of having my blood stirred, and I wish I was back in the Bowery! Something was always happening there I One day a fire, next day a fight, another day a fire and a fight together:' "Umph! and you to run with the engine I" "Don't talk about it, uncle 1 It makes me home sick I—every day something glorious to stir one's blood 1 Here nothing ever happens, hardly It has been three days since I caught Black Donald; ten days since you blowed up the whole household! Oh 1 I wish the barns would catch on fire! I wish thieves would break in and steal 1 I wish Demon's Run would only rise to a flood and play the demon for once! Oh—yah—oo /" said Cap, opening her mouth with a yawn, wide enough to threaten the dislocation of her jaws. " Capitola," said the old man, very gravely, "1 am getting seriously uneasy about you. I know I am a rough old soldier, quite unfit to educate a young girl, and that Mrs. Condiment can't manage you, and—Fu consult Mr. Goodwin!" he con- cluded, getting up and putting on his hat, and ,walking out of the breakfast room, where this con- versation had taken place. • Cap laughed to herself—" I hope it is not a sin! ' I know I should die of the blues if I couldn't give vent to my feelings—and tease uncle 1" Capitola had scarcely exaggerated her condition. The monotony of her life affected her spirits; the very absence of the necessity of thinking and caring for herself, left a dull void in her heart and brain; and as the winter waned, the annual spring fever of lassitude and dejection to which mercurial organi- sations like her own are subject, tended to increase the malady that Mrs. Condiment termed "a lowness of spirits." At his wit's end, from the combined feelings of his responsibility and his helplessness in his ward's case, Old Hurricane went and laid the matter before the Rev. Mr. Goodwin. Having reached the minister's house, and found him alone and disengaged in the library, Old Hur- ricane first bound him over to strict secrecy, and then "made a clean breast of it ;" told him where Capi- tola had been brought up, and under what circum- stances he had fouad her The honest ceuntry clergyman was shocked beyond all immediate power cf recovering himself —so shocked, in fact, that Old Hurricane, fearing he had gone too far, hastened to say: "But mind, on my truth as a man, my honour as a soldier, and my faith as a Christian, I declare that that wild, reckless, desolate child has passed unscathed through he terrible ordeal of destitution, poverty and exposure! She has, sir! She is as innocent as the most daintily sheltered young heiress in the county 1 she is, sir! and I'd cut off the tongue and ears of any man that said otherwise." "I do not say otherwise, my frier d 1 but I say that she has suffered a frightful series of perils." "She has come out of them safe, sir! I know it by a thousand signs I—what I fear for her is the future! I can't manage her! She won't obey me, except when she likes 1 she has never been taught obedience or been accustomed to subordination, and don't understand either! She rides and walks out alone in spite of all I can do or say 1 If she were a boy. I'd thrash her! But what can I do with a girl 1" said Old Hurricane, in despair. "Lock her up in her chamber until she is brought to reason," suggested the minister. " Demmy, she'd jump out of the window and break her neck! or hang herself in her garters! or starve herself to death! you don't know what an untam- able thing she is! Some birds, if caged, beat them- selves to death against the bars of their prison she is just such a wild bird as that 1" "Humph lit is a difficult case to manage; but you should not shrink from responsibility; you should be firm with her." "That's just what I can't be with the witch, confound her! she is such a wag, such a droll, such a mimic; disobeys roe in such a mocking, cajoling, affectionate way! I could not give her pain if her soul depended on it." "Then you should talk to her! try moral 'mason." "Yes, if I could only get her to be serious long enough to listen to me 1 But you see Cap isn't sentimental! and if / try to be, she laughs in my face 1"

"But then is she so insensible to all the benefits you have conferred upon her 2—will not gratitude influence her?" . _ _

"Yes; so far as repaying me with a genuine affection, fervent caresses and careful attentions to my little comforts can go! but Cap evidently thinks that the restriction of her liberty is too heavy a price to pay for protection and support! The little rogue I Think of her actually threatening, in her good humoured way, to cite me before the nearest justice to show cause why I detain her in my house!" "Well, you could easily do that, I suppose, and she could no longer oppose your anthority." "No, that is just what I couldn't do —I couldn't show any legal right to detain Capitoia." "Humph, that complicates the case very much." "Yes; and much more than you think! for I wish to keep Capitola until she is of legal age. I do not wish that she should fall into the hands of her perfidicus guardian until I shall be able to bring legal proof of his perfidy." "Then it appears that this girl has received ford play from her friends'?" "Foul play! I should think so Gabriel Le Noir has very nearly put his neck into a halter." "Gabriel Le Noir! Colonel Le Noir! Our neighbour !" exclaimed the minister. "Exactly so I—Parson I you have given me your word as a Chrestian rtnnister to be silentsforever concerning this interview or until I give you leave to speak of it." "Yes, Major. and I repeat my promise; but indeed, sir, you astound me !" "Listen 1" and let astonishment rise to conster- nation. I will tell you who Capitola is. You, sir, have been in this neighbourhood only ten years, and consequently you know Gabriel Le Noir only as the proprietor of Hidden House, a widower with one grown son--" --" And as a gentleman of irreproachable repu- tation, in good standing both in the church and in the county." "Exactly. A man that pays his pew-rent, gives good dintsers, and takes off his hat to women and clergymen. Well, sir, this gentleman of irreproach- able manners and mot als—this citizen of considera- tion in the community—this member in good stand- ing with the Church has qualified himself for a twenty years' residence in the penitentiary, even if not for the exultation of a hangman's halter." " Sir, I am inexpressibly shochted to hear you say So; and I must still believe that there is some great mistake." "Wait until I tell you: I, Ira Wa.rfield, have known Gabriel Le .Noir as a villain for the last eighteen yens. I tell you so without scruple, and hold myself ready to maintain my words in field or forum, by sword or law. Well, havin; known him so long, for such a knave, I was in no manner sur- prised to discever some six months ago that he was also a criminal, and only needed exposure to be- come a felon." "Sir, sir, this is strong language I" "I am willing to back it with 'life, liberty, anei sacred honour,' as the Declaration of Independence has it. Listen: Some sixteen Nears ago, before you came to take this pastoral charge, the Hidden House was occupied by old Victor Le Noir, the father of E'useene the heir, and of Gabriel the pre- sent usurner. The old man died, leaving a will to this effect : the landed estate, including the coal and iron mines, the Hidden House, and all the negroes, stock, furniture and other personal pro- perty upon the premises, to his eldest son Eugene, witla this proviso; that it Eugene should die without issue. the landed estate:, house, nsroea, etc.,

snould descend to his younger brother Ciabriel. To Gabriel he left his bank-stock and blessing." "An equitable will," observed the minister. "Yes; but hear. At the time of his father's death Eugene was travelling in Europe. On receiv- ing the news he immediately returned home, bring- ing with him a lovely young creature, a mere child, that he presented to his astounded neighbours as Madame Eugene Le Noir. I declare to you there was one simultaneous outcry of shame, that he should have trapped into matrimony a creature so infantile—for she was scarcely fourteen years of age." "It was indeed highly improper," said the minister."

_ "So thought all the neighbourhood; but when they found out how it happened, disapproval was changed to commendation. She was the daughter of a French pa' riot. Her father and mother had both perished on the scaffold in the sacred cause of liberty; she was thrown helpless, friendless, and penniless upon the cold charity of the world; Provi- dence cast her in the way of our sensitive and en- thusiastic young traveller. He pitied her; he loved her • and was casting about in his own mind how he could help without compromising her, when the news of his father's illness summoned him home.

Then, seeing no better way of protecting her, after a little hesitation on account of her tender years, he married her and brought her with him?' "Good deeds we know, must be rewarded in heaven, since on earth they are so often punished." "He did not long enjoy his bride. She was just the most beautiful creature that ever was seen—with

a promise of still more glorious beauty in riper years. I have seen handsome women and pretty women, but Madame Eugene Le Noir was the only perfectly beautiful woman I ever saw in my long life. My own rged_eyes_ seemed enriched'

only to look at he She adored Eugene, too— anyone could see that. At first she spoke Eng- lish broken music;' but soon her accent became as perfect as if she had been native- .. .•

born—how Could it have been otherwise when her

teacher and inspirer was Love? She won all hearts with her loveliness I—Humph! hear me, an old fool —worse, and Old Hurricane, betrayed into dis- course of love and beauty, merely by the remem- brance of Madame Eugene Le Noir I Ali, bright, exotic flower she did not bloom long. The bride

had scarcely settled down into the wife when one night Eugene Le Noir did not come home as usual. The next day his deal body, with a bullet in his brain, was found in the woods around the Hidden House. The murderer was never discovered. Gabriel Le Noir came in haste from the military post where be had been stationed. Madame Eugene was never seen abroad after the death of her husband. It was reported that elle had lost her reason—a consequence that surprised no one. Eugene having died without issue, and his young widow being mad, Gabriel, by the terms of his father's will, stepped at once into the fah possession of the whole property." "Something of all this I have heard before," said the minieter. "Very likely ; for thee facts and falsehoods were the common property of the neighbourhood. Bat what you have not heard before, and what is not known to any now living, except the criminals, the victims and myself, is, that three months after the death of her -husband, Madame Eugene La Noir gave birth to twins—one living, one dead. The dead child was privately buried; the living one, together with the nurse, that was the sole witness of the birth, was abduck ed." "Great heaven, can this be true!" exclaimed the minister, shocked beyond all power of self- control.

"True as gospel I I have proof enough to Carry Conviction to any honest breast—to satisfy any caviller—ezciept a court of justice. You shall hear. You remember the dying woman whom you dragged me out in the snow-storm to sue—blame you!" "Yes."

"She, was the abducted nurse, escaped and re- turned It was to tuaae a deposition to the facts I am about to relate, that she sent you to fetch me," said Old Harricane ; and with that he com- menced and related the whole dark history of crime comprised in the nurse's dying deposition. They examined the instrument together, and Old Hurricane again related, in brief, the incidents of his hurried journey to New York ; his meeting and identifying Capitola, and bringing her home in safety to his house. "And thus," said the old roan, "you perceive that this child whose birth was feloniously con- cealed, and who was cast away to perish among the wretched beggars, thieves and street-walkers of New York, is really the only living child of the late Eugene Le Noir, and the sole isheritrix of the Hidden House, with its vast acres of fields, forests, iron and coal-mines, water-powers, steam-mills, furnaces and foundries—wealth that I would not undertake to estimate within a million of dollars ! all of which is now held and enjoyed by that usurp- ing villain, Gabriel Le Noir!" "But," said the minister, gravely, "you a-7-?, of course, commenced proceedings on the part of your protegee." "Listen. I will tell you what I have done. When I first brought Cap home, I was moved not only by the desire of wreaking vengeance upon a most atrocious miscreant who had done ME an irrepar- able injury, but also by sympathy tor the little witch who had woo my heart at Fast sight. There- fore you may judge I lost no time in preparing to strike a double blow which should rain my own mortal enemy, and reinstate my favourite in her rights. With this view, immediately on my return home I sent for Breefe, my confidential attorney, and laid the whole matter before him." "And he--" "To my dismay he told me that though the case was clear enough, it was not sufficiently strong, in a legal point of view, to justify us in bringing suit; for that the dying deposition of the mulatto nurse could not be received as evidence in our courts." "You knew that before, sir, I presume." "Of course I did; but I thought it was a lawyer's business to get over such ditEcti, ties ; and I assure you, parson, that I flew into a pas:ion, and cursed court and law and lawyers to my heart's content! I would have quarrelled with old Breefe, then and there, only Breefe won't get excited. He very coolly advised me to keep the matter close, and my eye open, and gather all the corroborative testimony I could find, and that in the kneantime he would reflect upon the hest manner of proceeding." "I think, MAjor Warfiald, that his counsel was wise and disinterested. But tell me, sir, of the girl's mother I Is it not astonishing ;in fact, is it not perfectly iccomprehensible, that so lovely a woman as you have represented her to. be, should have consente(l to the concealment, it not to the destruction of her own legitimate offspring." "Sir to me it is not incomprehensible at all! She was at once an orphan and a widow; a stranger in a strange land; a poor, desolate, broken-hearted child, in the power of the cunningest and most unscrupulous villain that the Lord ever suffered to live! I wonder at nothing that he might have deceived or frightened her into doing l' "Heaven forgive us I Have I known that man for ten years to hear this account of him at last! But tell me, sir, have you really any true idea of what has been the fate of the poor young widow?" "No—not the slightest. Immediately after his brother's funeral, Gabriel Le Noir gave out that Diadem° Eugene had lost her reason through excessive grief, soon after which he took her with him to the North, and upon his return alone, reported that he had left her in a celebrated Lunatic Asylum. The story was probable enough, and received universal belief. Only now Ido not credit it, and do not know whether the widow be living or dead; or if living, whether she be mad or sane; if dead, whether she came to her end by fair means or foul I" "Merciful Heaven, sir! you do not mean to say—" "Yes, I do mean to say; and if you would like to know what is on my private mind I'll tell you. I believe that Madame Eugene Le Noir has been treacherously made away with by the same infernal demon at whose instigation her husband was mur- dered and her child stolen." The minister seemed crushed beneath the over- whelming weight of this communication ; he passed his hand over his brow, and thenee down Ins face, and sighed deeply; for a few moments be seemed unable to reply, and when he spoke it was only to

say: "In this matter, Major Warfield, I can offer you no counsel better than that of your confidential attorney—follow the light that you have, until it lead you to the full elucidation of this affair, and may Heaven grant that you may find Colonel Le Noir less guilty than you apprehend." "Parson I—humbug When charity drivels it ought to be turned off by justice I I will follow the little light I have! I suspect .frpm the description, N that the wretch who at Le oir's instance carried off the nurse and child, was no other than the notorious 131ack Donald. I have offered an addi- tional thousand dollars for his apprehension, and if he is taken he will be condemned to death, make a last dying speech and confession, and give up his accomplices, the accomplished Colonel Le Noir among the rest!" "If the latter really was an accomplice, there could be no better way of discovering the fact than to bring this Black Donald to justice ; but I greatly fear that there is little hone of that!

"CAPITAL" °TINT-

Why Dagland is hated by the other nations of the world has of late become a much-debated ques- tion. But it is by no means a new one. There has come into my hands a letter written by the late Mr. Roebuck about the year 1860, in which he uses language singularly appropriate to to-day. "I know not," he wrote, "whether you have remarked that though we exercise a mighty and I will say a bene- ficial influence upon all the affairs of Europe,there is a marked hate of everything English in the minds of most of the rulers or Europe. Nowhere is this more shown, in spite of all alliances, than in Prussia. This dislike, this hate, is caused by two things—our freedom and our success. Our success is also twofold: we have been successful in war, but we have been and are successful in commerce. Now despotic governors hate our freedom; and the people of Europe, under the influence of despotism and iguorance,hate our commercial success. In no country has this feeling been more manifest than in Germany. The legislation of the Zollverein has been more hostile and directly hostile to England than has been that of France; and an Englishman is treated with far more courtesy in France than he is in Germany."

Mr. Sexton's refusal to accept the chairmanship of the Irish Parliamentary party has been received with deep and widespread disappointment by the members of the.party generally. To many of them the final decision of the member for North Kerry has come with the force of an unexpected blow, as the moderate men of both sections were sanguine that Mr. Sexton would see the necessity of making ' a sacritce for the sake of the 'unity of the party, and the safeguarding of the cause of Irish nationa- lity. That he has failed to realise the depth of the desire of the Irish people as expressed through their representatives is a bitter surprise to his col- leagues, and in many the feeling amounts to one of resentment. It is believed that this unfortunate decision will have grave consequences to the party. It is regrettable on all accounts, as it practically means the withdrawal of Mr. Sexton from Parlia- mentary life, and this will ben great loss to the House of Ccmmons. There seems now to be no in- surmountable obstacle to the accession of Mr. Dillon to the, chair.

The steamboat service of the river Thames has recently *teen the subject of i-aquiry, with the re- sult that it is clear that if we are to have an effici- ent service and restore the Thames to its old posi- tion as a highway the piers must be freed from toll, just as the bridges have been, and the landing- stages be improved SO as to give better approaches. If the requirements of the passenger traffic of the' river are not met by private enterprise the County Council will probably provide a service.

With reference to the sudden departure from London of Mr. Rhodes, the ex-Cape Premier, it may be mentioned that those who ,knew him best were the least surprised. Mr. Rhodes is a man of action, and delights in springing surprises on the public. He is fond of travel, and is so used to going long distances that he would think less of a journey of thousands of miles than would .the average Londoner of a run to Brighton. His ultimate destination,is, most likely, Rhodesia,and in Buluwayo he will pro- bably put up at the mansion built for him on the site of Lobenguela's " palace:' The natives, with their keen appreciation of the significance of events have 'christened Mr. Rhodes's new home with a name indicating " he who sits down " in the place of aaother.

It might be mentioned in connection with Presi- dent Kruger's proposed visit to England that he entered upon his seventy-fist year on October ICth lastile:is,therefore,an old man to undertake so long a ,journey, but, according to all reports, he retains much of his physical act;vity. The President's birth- day is one of the stated annual holidays directed to be observed by the Transvaal Government. Another anniversary observed ae a holiday relates to the de- feat of the great chief Dingaan in 1838. President 'Kruger was, of course; in England in connection with the settlement .which followed Majuba,.and then—as he will be again—he was accompanied by .a.group of colleagues. It will be noted with-some interest that he is a knight of the German order of the Red Eagle, and a 'commander of the Legion of Honour. He is also a member of a Portuguese order, and of the order of the Netherlands Lion. Will he be offered:an English decoration when he comes to London?

It is not often that a scientific society in London has to announce" house full," but at Mr. Swinton's demonstration of the Rontgen process before the Royal Photographic-Eociety on Tuesday night .crowds were turned away. Tha lecturer began, by illustrating the effect c f the ordinary electricitis- charge upon a sensitive plate. From that he passed to the vaccum or Geissler tube, which he exhausted while the, current was illuminating it, and showed in conclusion a brilliant collection of Crookes tubes phosphorescing with rays of all colours, according to the nature of their contents. Amongst the most beatztiful were a group of rubies which the cathode rays caused to glow with a deep red light. Photographs were shown in the lantern of the bones of the hand, of a foot. of a live 'frog, and of a sole, and a number of the audience handed up their purses to the platform in order that their contents might be photographed without the purses being opened. A visitor with a splintered bone in the middle of his left hand also submitted to the operation, which•resulted most successfdily, the bones of the entire hand being very clearly ren- clered. The Earl of Crawford, Mr. J. W. GiKord, and .Captain Abney, among others, discussed the tre- sults, and commented,very warmly on the success of the demonstration.

EXPLOSION .OF AN AEROLITE OVER

MADRID, SERIOUS PANIC IN THE CITY. MADRID, MONDAY.—At half-past nine this morning an aerolite of considerable size exploded in the atmosphere above Idadrid. The phenomenon was accompanied ,tiy a vivid glare of blinding light,followed by a loud report., which caused a general panic among the people. All the buildings in the city were shaken, and many windows were smashed by the concussion. The Madrid Observa- tory states that the explosion occurred at a height of 20f miles above the earth's surface. The sky was clear and the sun was shining brightly at the time of the explosion, all that was visible in the heavens being a white cloud bordered with red, which was travelling eastward at a great rate. The panic was general throughout the city. especially in the markets. Many persons,fainted,and the superstitious populace be- lieved that the occurrence was-significant of the Divine wrath. At the.Observatery it was believed that frag- ments of the .meteor fell somewhere in the suburbs, and a party was sent out to search for-them. At the tobacco manufactory there was a stampede among the work- women,who imagined that an earthquake had occurred. They all rushed pell-mell down a staircase, which gave way beneath the exceptional weight. Seventeen of them were injured, one of them,it is believed, fatally. Other accidents are reported from various quarters of the city. A young man in his terror lumped from a first- floor window and was badly hurt. an nearly all the schools and colleges the pupils threw down their books and made for the doors, and in the frantic struggles to escape many were seriously bruised. Many shopkeepers closed their establishments. The Palace did nor escape the general excitement. It was feared at first that a bomb had been exploded,but the King's tutor on learn- ing the real nature of the occurrence reassured the Court. Not only were buildings shaken,but at least one house is known to have collapsed. At the United States Legation a partition wall fell in and many of the windows were shattered,but no other damage was done to the building. L great many houses throughout the city sustained similar damage, partition walls being shaken down by the force of the explosion. The excitement in the city, especially in the suburbs, has not yet calmed down. The explosion was heard over a distance of several kilornetros from Madrid. At Guadalajara,a town about forty-six miles from the capital, the explosion was very strongly felt. Several small pieces of greyish stone, still warm, have been picked up in Madrid. Later reports of the phenomena attending the passage of the aerolite which burst over Madrid, state that at Valencia the aerolite was observed in the form of a ball of fire purging swiftly through the air in the direction of the capital. A similar appearance was noticed at La Corunna and near Jaen, with the addition that the pro- jectile seemed to cast a line of light alcrig its course. At Burgos two aerolites, probably fragments of the main body, fell on the town with a loud noise, the second ex- ploding in the air at nine o'clock in the morning, to the great alarm of the inhabitants.At Logreno three meteoric bolts burst in mid air, incandescent fragments falling into the town, causing the outbreak of two fires.

"THE WAIIDERINGS OF AN ATOM."

',LECTURE AT BOLD-SrREET "zWESLEYAN SCHOOL-

ROOM.

Another lecture was given at the Bold-st. Wesleyan Schoolroom on Monday evening, in connection with the Wesley Guild, entitled "The wanderings, of an atom," illustrated by emperiments. 'rho lecturer was Mr. Alfred Hutchinson,B.A. (Canteb), B. Sc. (Lonclon),and recently science master ,at the Leys, Camtnidge. The chair was occupied by Dr. J. Guest Gornall, and there was a very good attendance. The Re. G. D. Mason,in a few intro- ductory remaiLs, said that they met there that night one who was very well known by name in Warrington, and the other was a comparative stranger, though he was known to some. Dr. Gornall was very well known in A'arrington and it was a very great pleasure to meet I him that night. He had promied to give a lecture but I could not do-co on account of his very sad illness. They all rejoiced that he was so far recovered in health and strength, and he (Mr. Mason) hoped that he would be stronger, and that the career to which he had devoted himself would be an exceedingly happy and successful one. Gornall) he..d gone on with medical work, and by a strange stroke of fate had found himself at last landed back into his native town. A SPORTING CASE.

A sporting case of some interest came before Judge Collier at the Liverpool County County Court on Mon- day, George Cain, of Bolton, claimed £5O from the pro- prietors of the" Sporting Chronicle," Manchester, that being the amount of stakes deposited with the defen- dants in connection with a boxing contest between two men named respectively Brierley and Vaughan. The plaintiff found the money on behalf of Brierley, on a written condition that if the latter won the plaintiff would receive the stakes, Brierley getting only the " purse" offered for the winner. Brierley won the fight and came so Manchester and claimed the stakes, wbich Vvere paid to him. The plaintiff contended that ale de- fradents had no right to pa'y the money over, and that ' they should have handed it to him. His Honour held that the transaction was in the nature of a bet, and that the plaintiff could not recover.

WHY ARE THE WAVES WASTED?

The amount of power in the forces of Nature which daily goes to waste is incalculable. The utilisation of the dynamic force of the tides is a subject which has engaged the minds of men for centuries, with little or no practical result. Another attempt has been made in this direction, in America, on the Rhode Island coast. A dam has been built across the mouth of a narrow bay, in which the tidal rise and fall is considerable. By means of a second dam, the bay is divided into two great reservoirs. The water stored in these is made to turn a wheel, which it is proposed to connect by suitable gear- ing to a dynamo for the generation of electricity. The belief is held in the best engineering circles that the problem of the commercial utilisation of tidal energy is rapidly approaching solution, anu there are indications that within the next two years a gigantic and carefully worked out scheme of this nature will be made public. Should such au enterprise prove successful, some sweep- ing developments in the supply of light and power to coastwise ctbles, and the transmission of the same to towns and villages in the neighbouring country, may be looked for.

===== 0002601_18960215_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
LITTLE GREAT MEN.

It is a romarkable thing that some of the greatest men in history have been of small stature. Certainly,from all experience, height of person has no influence over on the mental faculties. The chances seem to be that smallness of size, hi fact, at times, a little lameness, is advantage- ous. The reason for this is tolerably plain. Tall and robust men are apt to devote themselves to, or at least to derive so mach enjoyment from, boisterous pursuits as to be rather indiffereat to any speciality in mental culture. Men of small stature, and perhaps weak health, are, on the contrary,driven to mental occupation. Stud- ying hard in their several vocations, they rise to distinc- tion. A comforting reflection this for young men who have the misfortune to labour under personal infirmities. Here are a few notable examples of " little men." William the Malmesbury has preserved the tradition that one of the greatest of our English kings, Edgar the Pacific, was extremely both small in stature and in bulk. William declared it to be an opinion amongst the English in his own day (that is two centuries later than Edgar's reign) thas"no king either of his own or earlier times in .Ecaland could be justly and fairly compared to Edgar." The next king of the English whose might can be at all compared with Edgar's, was the Danish Canute; his kingdom, indeed, had a far wider range. It is curious that this great warrior and legislator was also a singu- larly small man. William of Malmesbury reports that when Edmund Ironside and Canute were facing each other intarms at Gloucester and everything was ready for battle, the Englishman asked the Dane to settle the contest by a single combat, and try their fortune with- out the destruction of so many faithful adherents. Canute replied that his courage was' great enough, but that he was apprehensive of trusting his diminutive per- son against so bulky an antagonist. He proposed that they should neither fight each other, nor let their armies come to blows, but divide the kingdom, Edmund taking Wessex, and Canute taking Mercia. I have some suspicion, says the writer, that the monk of Malmesbury must himself have been rather dwarfish. for he is not only careful to take note of the smallness of size of great kings, but he has an admiration for every appearance of a mighty mind in a little body.; he tells at length the story of a Norman knight named Balso, 'of small size, but incredible courage," who, in a fight out- side Pavia, in which more than a thousand men were en- gaged, was the only survivor on either side. This ter- rible little warrior hovered alone around the city, and by his single sword frightened the citizens as long as he thought proper. William tells us that St. Neot was so dwarfish that he had a step made of iron bars to stand upon while saying mass, in order to be able to reach the altar. It was kept at Glastonbury as a relic in his day. He has also been careful to notice that the mightiest of all the Popes, Gregory VIL (Hildebrand), was a diminu- tive man. Another great king of England, who, like Canute, was of a foreign,lalthough a kindred race, William 111. was of notoriously low stature. At the joint coronation of William and Mary,on Saturday, April 1.1. 1689, the short king and the tall queen walked side by side, not as sovereign and consort, but as joint sovereigns, with the sword between them; and the queer contrast between the figures a the imposing Stuart lady and the meagre little Dutch gentleman, did not escape the satirical obser- vation of the vexed Jacobites. Of the three great world-wide conquerors whom Napoleon classed together—himself, Alexander of Macedon, and Car—he alone was a little man. His predecessors were both of them men of a truly majectic presence, tall of stature. Alexander, indeed, if his por- traits are exact, was remarkable for his handsome and manly aspect. Bonaparte was always presented in the English caricatures of him.both pictorial and verbal,as a kind of pigmy. When the vulgar English crowded to his levees as First Consul at the Tuileries in 1802, after the peace of the preceding October, to the disgust of the high-minded Sir Samuel Romilly, they were probably disappointed at not finding him to be a dwarf. "Bona- parte," says Miss Berry, in her lively discription of one of his receptions, "by no means struck me tis so little as I had heard him represented, and as indeed he appeared on horseback. His shoulders are broad, which gives his figure importance." Allusions to his stature were not always received by him with complaisance ; but there is some humour in a correction which he once administered , to one of his imperial grand chamberlains. The Emperor had made several fruitless attempts upon tiptoe to reach a book placed on a high shelf in his cabinet. The official, hurrying eagerly to his assistance, said awkwardly "permit me, sire ; lam greater than your Majesty" , (Jo suis plus grand que votre Majeste). "Please to say you are longer " (Dites, done, plus long), said Napoleon, with a scornful smile. An earlier victorious French soldier, whose name is invariably cited as the" Great Conde," was a little man; so was his admiring pupil. the Duke of Luxembourg, of whom William of Orange once angrily said: " I can never beat that little hunchback !" " How does he know I am a hunchback 1' " said Luxembourg, on hearing of the exclamation. " I have often seen his back, but he has never yet seen mine." The most celebrated of all our naval English heroes, Nelson, was none the less dear for his small size. The first of Russian warriors, the strange Suwarrow, was another of those leaders who shortness of physical statue seems to be reflected in the short decisiveness of their actions, according to the proverb, "Little and quick." Suwarrow said that all his victorious tactics could be compressed into two words—,' Advance ' • strike." He was famous, also, for the laconism of his despatches, like many earlier and later commanders of the first rank. Whenever he held a conversation, he studied to express himselfjwitla great conciseness. It seems to be a fact, however, that great generals of small size do not always prefer to be followed by small soldiers. Imposing stature has usually been in demand for the rank and file of fighting-men. Marius would not willingly enlist any soldiers that were not six feet high. Mr. Carlyle has pictured with vivacity the tall Potsdam regiment of Frederick-William, "the great drill-sergeant of the Prussian nation." Aristotle says that the Ethiopians and Indians, in choosing their

kings and leaders, had particular regard to the beauty and statue of hheir persons. Perhaps the Greeks, with whom physical perfection counted for so much foll Owed the philosopher's great pupil, Alexander, with the more satisfaction for the splendour of his person. In the chapter on Magnanimity and Little-Mindedness in his " Ethics." :Aristotle lays it down as a principle, that beauty only exists with good statue; that we may call little persons pretty, but may not call them beautiful. Montaigne says that if a man of goodly stature marches at the bead of a battalion, it creates respect in those that follow,and is a terror to the enemy, but it is plain, from the instances already cited, that the lively Perigordin was reasoning " a priori." It appears, from a slightly querulous bit of self-portraiture in the essay on Pre- sumption, in which he makes this remark, that the essayist himself was somewhat below the middle stature, and keenly felt the insufficiency ; he says that his defective size "carries with it a great deal of incon- veniency ; for that authority which a graceful presence and a majestic mien beget, is wanting." It is not im- possible that the sense of his bodily littleness was one cause of the eagerness with which he gave himself to study and criticism. Many of the greatest wits and humorists have been but insignificant creatures in ap- pearance—for instance, Voltaire, Quevedo, and Scarreu. The last :called himself "an abridgment of human I miseries." Le Sage, who was singularly handsome, and Swift, who was a tall and muscular man, are witnesses that fli ! keenest wit is got confined to a small bodily , lodging. Both Dryden and Pope were little men. When Calvin arrived at Nerac, and was trying to find the great-hearted Lefevre, every one of whom he made inquiries gave him the some sort of answer : " Lelevre is a little bit of man,' but lively as gunpowder." This Lefevre was quick to perceive the destiny of the young iequirer,and was the first to prophesy his future import- ance in the history of religion among the French-speak- ing peoples.

Mr. Thomas Sexton, M.P., has finally declined to ac- ept the chairmanship .of the Irish Parliamentary Party.

WARRINGTON CHAMBER OF

COMMERCE.

ANNUAL MEETING. The 19th annual meeting of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce was held at Market Gate Chambers on Monday morning. Mr. J. H. Fleming, J.P., occupied the chair, and there were also present—Councillor W. Pierpoint, J.P., Councillor H. Roberts, J.P., Mr. R. Garnett, J.P., Mr. W. Long, J.P., Mr. F. Monks, J.P., C.C., J. Fairclough, J.P., and Councillor A. Bennett (secretary).

ANNUAL REPORT.—THE SHIP CANAL. The following report of the proceedings of the past year was submitted :—" The Council are pleased to be able to give the following record of some of the work done during the year as evidence of the utility of the Chamber: The president and vice-president were ap- point3d as delegates to the annual meeting in London of the Associated Chambers. A considerable number of Certificates of Origin have been issued by the Chamber during the year to local manufacturers trading with the various centinental countries in which such certificates are required. The questions addressed by the commit- tee to the Associated Chamber of Commerce on the im- portant subject of Companies Acts (Amendment) Com- mittee were very carefully considered, and the replies printed and embraced in the evidence which was brought before the committee. The Council proposed that the law should be strengthened in the direction of prevent- ing the issue of fraudulent prospectuses, the registraticm of companies before a proper proportion of the capital had been subscribed, and the mortgaging of the floating assets of a company without registration. ; also that the Audit Regulations in Table A should be incorporated in the Companies Acts themselves. There is reason to hope that effective legislation will follow the inquiry. The Council much regret to have to report the death of Mr. Joseph Davies, and unanimously passed the follow- ing resolution :—" That this Chanaber expresses its deep regret at the death of Mr. Joseph Davies, J.P., who was one of the founders of the Chamber, and took an active part in its affairs for many years ; and desires to tender its respectful sympathy to Mrs. Davies and the family, in the loss which they have sustained thereby." The President, Vice-President, and Messrs. John Fairclough, William Pierpoint, Henry Roberts, and the Secretary were specially appointed to attend the funeral. The Council have closely followed every step in the negotia- tions between the Corporations and the Ship Canal Corn. pony, and have been able to materially assist in the amicable settlement which is now anticipated: They hope to be able to publish a full report of the proceed- ings in due course. The Chamber is now affiliated with the Mansion House Association on Railway and Canal Traffic, and has from time to time made repre3entations to it in the interest of local trade, as well as supplying local manufacturers with the forms which are required in case of appeal against excessive charges. The Coun- cil have on several occasions had the metric question before them. They made a recommendation to the Select Committee appointed to consider it in favour of the early adoption of the system, and subsequently ap- pointed Mr. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., to represent theirs on the deputation which Mr. Balfour received on the subject, when he declared that, although he did not con- sider the time ripe for compulsory legislation, he agreed with the deputation as to the desirability of legalizing the system, and thoroughly teaching it in all public schools. Both Houses of Parliament have been memorialised (through Lord Cross and Mr. Pierpoint) during the year in favour of the acquisition by the State of the telephonic system of the United Kingdom, and its management as a department of the Post Office. Dir. J. Fulton Smith attended a Council meeting (on behalf of Messrs Peter Walker and Sons, Limited) to suggest that, in order to avert the establishment of a School Board in Warrington, the Chamber should co-operate with the leading local manufacturers in arranging that a definite sum should be set aside by each of them,to be applied as might be requisite,for the purpose of assisting the Volun- tary Schools to comply with the wishes of the Education Department; but, as there was some,diSference of opinion upon the subject, no action was taken in the matter. Replies were sent to several questions received from Sir Albert Kaye Rollit, the President of the London Chamber, as to the state of local trade. The Council wish to again call the attention of the members to the interesting blue books, pamphlets, statistics, and othercommercial litera- ture which is forwarded to the Secretary,and which may at any time be seen on application, and cordially invite any local firms who are not yet connected with the Chamber to strengthen its influence and still further pro- mote its utility by joining. J. H. FLEMING, president; ARTHUR BENNETT, hon. secretary."—The Secretary said that he had intended to incorporate in the report a detailed account of the negotiations for the recent settle- ment with the Ship Canal. He thought it was suffici- ently interesting to set forth the part taken by the Chamber. He had seen Councillor Roberts and the Town Clerk about it, however, and they thought that as the matters were not definitely settled, it would be better at not to mention them. He therefore would issue a sup- plementary report later on, showing the part the Chamber had taken.—Councillor Roberts said that he could not understand a statement which appeared in one of the papers on Saturday to the effect that the terms of the negotiations had been published and had been pub- licly discussed in the Council Chamber, and everything had now been completed except purely formal proceed- ings.—Councillor Bennett: Except the docks. I don't think the actual terms as to the docks have been pub- lished.—Councillor Roberts said that he considered the question of the docks as of secondary importance. He was now referring to the negotiations which had taken place and the agreements which had been entered into. They had been set fully before the public and those had been nothing withheld from the public.—Mr. F. Monks, as one who was very much interested in the success of the negotiations, said that he thought they all owed a great debt to Mr. Roberts for the manner in which he had conducted them. He thought a very fair bargain had been made. He thought is was very fair and reason- able.—Mr. Long said he was very pleased to see tha new departure in the setting forth of the report which was very much more voluminous and detailed the work done by the Chamber during the past year. He dared say that for that they had to thank their secretary. He hoped that copies of the report would be sent to every manufacturer in the town who was not a subscriber to the Chamber.—Mr. John Fairclough also expressed a similar desire, and the Secretary said that he would do so.—The report was then adopted on the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. W. Long.

Andrew Rathbone, William Carson, John Fairclough, Peter Stnbs, Linnsess Greening, T.A. Hemp- son,T. J.Hulse. John flutchinge,William Long,F.Monks, IL A. Naylor, William Pierpoint,J. H. Fleming,and John Houghton, The various officers for the past year were also thanked for their services. The president, vice- president, and any member who cared to attend, were appointed delegates to the annual meeting of Associated Chambers of CoMMerCe, to be held at Guildhall, London, on March 24, 25, and 26. PROPOSED NAVY LEAGUE.

A letter was read from the Secretary of the Navy League, suggesting that the Chamber of Commerce at Warrington constitute itself a local branch of the League. The objects of the League were stated to be non-political, and to be for the purpose of keeping the people well informed in navy matters,as it was very im- portant that the navy of England should be kept in an efficient state for the protection of commerce.—Mr.Fair- clough : What is the Navy League to do P—The Secre- tary : It will really be educational.—Mr. Monks : I thought it was in the way of training.—Councillor Pier- point : It is to prod the Chancellor of the Exchequer to spend more millions.—The Secretary : Another ob- ject appears to be to get the Colonies to bear their fair share in the expense of the navy.—Mr.Monks : I thiuk we want more information before we can say whether we will join or not.—The Secretary said that the navy was a sort of insurance for their interests all over the world. Ile would write to the Secretary to get the rules, of the League.—The matter then dropped. SUGGESTED EXHIBITION OF LOCAL MANUFACTURES.

The SECRETARY said that he had received a letter trom a gentleman who did not wish his name to transpire, in which he said that having regard to the many varieties of manufactures which were carried on in Warrington, it would be interesting and instructive to get the museum authorities to co-operate with the Chamber in getting a representative collection of the principal local manufactures. There were about 27 different trades in Warrington.—Councillor Pierpohat : Tnere are 33 ' or 35. Warrington is a most remarkable town for that.— Mr., Monks said that if that scheme were carried out, a new wing would be wanted for the museum.—Coun- cillor Roberts thought the matter well worth considering but how they were to get it carried out, he did not know. Want of space and want of money were in the way. The committee had not a penny to spare, but it seemed to him that there could be no two opinions about the desirability of the suggestion if it could be carried out. If some one would leave them £lO,OOO they might find room to spare.—Mr. Monks said it was the jubilee of the Corporation next year,and perhaps then an exhibition of local industries might be held in connec- tion with it.—After further discussion, it was moved by Mr. Monks, seconded by Mr. Long, and carried, that the Secretary be requested to write to the Mayor that in the opinion of that Chamber it was desirable that an exhi- bition be held in connection with the jubilee of the Cor- poration.

This concluded the business.

The Company by the by were large exhibi- tors at the Liverpool Cycle Exhibition last week.J
