THE QUEEN'S SPEECH.

The second session of the Queen's fourteenth Parlia- ment was opened on Tuesday. The Queen's Speech. read by the Lord Chancellor in the Hones of Lords, re- ferred to the agreement with France in regard to Siam, the delimitation of the boundary between Afghanistan and India and Russia,the wish expressed by the Ameri- can Government to co-operate in-terminating the differ- ences which have existed for years:between England and Venezuela,and the adoption by the Sultan of reforms in the government of the Armenian provinces. where" a fanatical outburst on the part of a section of the Turkish population has resulted in a series of massacres which have caused the deepest indignation in this country." A searching inquiry was promised into the sudden in- cursion of an armed force into the South African Re- public,and the satisfaction expressed at the success of the Ashanti expedition,her Majesty at the same time de- ploring the loss, from the severities of the climate, of some valuable lives, "including that of my beloved son-in-law Prince Henry of Battenberg." The Estimates for the year, it was stated, had been pre- pared with the utmost regard to economy, " but the exigencies of the time require an increased expenditure." The Speech then recapitulated the chief matters to which the attention of Parliament is to be specially directed. Lord Stanmore moved and the Earl of Roslyn seconded the Address in reply. Lord Rosebery then spoke. Upon the Transvaal he observed that it -Ras unfortunate, if the Government wished to dissociate themselves from Dr. Jameson's raid, that they should have taken occasion very shortly after it took place to print, publish, and circulate, in their official organ and through their official poet.a glaring eulogy on the enterprise. Touching next on Venezuela' his Lerd- ship regarded the intervention of the United States as a satisfactory incident, because it introduced into the controversy the important element of a solid and sub- stantial Government which afforded a guarantee, on the other side, for the permanence of any settlement arrived at. On the subject of Armenia Lord Rosebery hoped to receive from the Government more copious information. In concluding his remarks he said lie could not believe that all had been done in the matter of Armenia that could be done. Lord Salisbury, in reply, said with re- ference to Venezuela and the United States that he had had an increasing belief during the last few weeks that we should—tbough it might not be till after long negoti- ation—find some settlement of the question between England and the States, and that all danger of any rup- ture would be entirely removed, with the aid of com- bined negotiation and arbitration. But they had not yet arrived at any agreement. Respecting Armenia, Lord Salisbury maintained that no treaty existed pledging us to go to war with the Sultan if he did not govern his country better. The powers were by no means inclined to tolerate a military occupation on our part. To satisfy Lord Rosebery's demands England would have had first to undertake a military occupation without the military power, second to push that occupation against 300,000 Turkish soldiers, and, third, would have bad to meet a danger. which he did not wish to define more closely, in the disapproval of the European Powers. But the Government entirely repudiated the idea that they had abandoned the Armenians, or that the Concert of Europe would be of no advantage to the Christians of the Turkish Empire. The Address was agreed to, and the House rose. _ . . _ _

!T.7IIVMVW.:

In the House of Commons the Speaker took the chair at two o'clock,and after members had heard the Queen's Speech read in the House of Lords, a brief adjournment took place. When the House met again a new writ was ordered for the Montrose Burghs. The following notices of motion were given by members of the Govern- ment :—Mr. Goschen, a bill to make provision for the construction of works for the purposes of the Royal Navy ; Mr. Chaplin, a bill to amend the law with res- pect to the rating of occupiers of agricultural land ; Mr. Long, a bill to amend the law relating to agricultural holdings, to make further provision in respect of loans for agricultural purposes, and to amend the Diseases Animals Act: Mr. G. Balfour, a bill to further amend the law relating to the occupation and ownership of land in Ireland ; Mr. Ritchie, a bill to facilitate the construc- tion of light railways,also a bill to make better provision for the settlement of trade disputes ; Sir M. White Rid- ley,a'bill to amend the law in respect to compensation to workmen in case of injuries arising in the couree of em- ployment ; Sir J. Gorst, a bill to make further provision for education in England and Wales. The Address in reply to the Speech from the Throne was moved by Mr. G.J. Goschen, jun.,and seconded by Sir J. Stirling Max- well. Sir W.llarcourt said that never in his recollection had the House of Commons assembled under such criti- cal circumstances as it did that day. Lie rejoiced to think that the paragraph dealing with our relations with the United States held out the hope that that question was on the road to an immediate settlement. It was painfully apparent that there had been misunderstandings both in England and America on this subject. There were only two modes of -settling the question. One was by an amicable Convention. If it should appear that the differences of opinion were not capable of solution In that way, what objection could there be to refer it to the arbitration of a third Power ? With regard to the Transvaal, there were reasons %doe it was impossible to discuss the question now. Two things however, could be said. In the first place, they could express their complete approval of the conduct of the Colonial Secretary ; and in the second,they could recog- nise the magnanimity and humanity which President Kruger had exhibited under circumstances of unparalle- led provocation. As to Arinesna, the language of the Queen's Speech was inadequate, totally unworthy, and entirely failed to represent the views of this country. Mr. A. J. Balfour said that, as to our dispute with America, neither he nor his friends had said that the Monroe Doctrine was one to which England had a right to object. The American Commission had asked the Eng- lish Government to aid them with any information they haa on the Venezuelan : question, and the Government had promised to give them the fullest possible informa- tion at the earliest possible moment. With regard to events in the Transvaal,a detailed statement of the posi- tion of the Chartered Company would no doubt be given by the Colonial Secretary, but the armed forces of the Company would be at once transferred to an Imperial officer, avid the Government intended that at the close •of the proceedings now pending a full inquiry should be entered upon into all the circumstances connected with the charter. The debate was continued by Mr. S. Smith, Sir H. Vincent, Sir C. Dilke, Mr. Bryce, and Mr. Goscheri.

DISTRIBUTION OF PRIZES AT TIIE BLUE COAT

SCHOOL.

The annual distribution of prizes took place nt the Warrington Blue Coat School last evening week. His Worship the Mayor distributed Me prizes, and there were also present the Mayoress, Mrs. Parr,the Rev. Canon Willis, the Rev. R. Greenall and family, the Rev. E.C.E. Carleton, and Mrs. Carleton, Mr. and ; Mrs. Hspherd, Mr. and Mrs. J. E. Davies, the Misses Litton, the Misses White, Miss Appleton, &c. An excellent programme of music and recitations was gone through by the children, under the conductor- ship of Mr. Rose, Mr. Armstrong conducting the selections by the band. Mr. Thomas Lane was ap- plauded for his performance of the organ solo. The programme was as follows :—Anthem, "Rejoice in the Lord"; solo and chorus, "Thou wilt keep him ". anthem," Let every heart rejoice " ; part song, " gentle breath of spring".' recitation, "Let's try to do the right, boys"; trio, "The evening bells " ; cornet solo, " Within the minster " ; recitation, " The farmer's wife " ; part song, Afar in the gloaming'; organ solo, by Mr. T. Lane, professor of music; re- elation," Hodge and the vicar".; song and chorus. "In the days of 'Good Queen Bess " ; quartette by the band, " 11 Trovatore " ; part song, " He climbs the mountain side"; recitation, " The maiden and the rainbow" ;trio, "The evening shadows "; recita- tion, "The Jackdaw of Rheims' ; euphonium solo. " Sissie " ; recitation, "Sixty years ego' song, "Song for Blue Coat boys and girls " ; recitation. "The fox in the well " ; duet," Sleep on, thou babe"; part song," With sound of my horn " ; quartett by the band, " Sanctus." The following is a list of prize winners :—T. H. Kettle, R. Ilughes,E Bonnell, T. Snelson, E. Jones, E. Lawless, H. 11 arding, G. W. Kettle, H. Hewitt, W. Broughton, E. Lawless M. A. Browne, M. E. Jordon, A. Woodhouse, J. Parting- ton, E. Dudley, P. S. Walker, M. J. Cooper, G. Winstanley, B. Ingham, W. Partington, and J. W. Walker. The Chairman, in the course of his re- marks, commented upon the excellence of the in- struction given in the schools, which rtflected the greatest credit upon the master and mistress, and advised the children to carry out in their after life the principles taught to them in the school. Before the last piece was performed votas of thanks were given to the Mayor for presiding and to the head master for the excellent entertainment provided.

SERVING INTOXICITED PERSONS WITH DRINK.

THE LICENSEE OF THE " SEIAKESPLIRE INN"'

FINED.

On Monday morning before the Mayor (chairman), Aldermen Harrison, Francomb, Councillors Roberts, and Pierpoint, Messrs. F. Monks,Dr. Gornall,J. W.Ha.yward, J. H. Fleming, and S. Wright, Margaret Green, widow, licensee of the Shakespeare Inn,was charged with having on the 20th January sold ale to Annie Walsh and James Walsh, persons then drunk,on her premises. The Chief Constable stated that on the 20th of Janu- nary Annie Walsh came to Warrington for the purpose of attending the trial of her husband at the Warrington Court where he was charged with stealing a watch. After the case was over she joined Thomas Walsh, her brother-in-law and they seemed to have been through the town drinking from public-house to public-house and got fairly on the spree. She attempted to pawn some clothes at the Tower, Bridge-street, but the manager ordered her out as she was drunk, and on mak- ing a disturbance in the street P.C.Bell sent her away.A. quarter of an hour after Bell saw her with James Walsh going up Dolman's-lane. As he had heard of her pawn- ing a now gold ring,he in company with Sergeant Smith followed them. They went in the "Shakespeare Inn," and as the officers passed they looked in through the window and saw the two Walehes sitting in the baga- telle room. The officers knew the place and were aston- ished to find that before each of the Walshes was a glass of beer pirt!y consumed. They went in and called Mrs.. Green's attention to the condition of her two customers. She seemed astonished and said " I did not notice that tbey were so bad when they came." Sue then added "I will give them their money back and take the beer away," thus practically admitting that they were .drunk. She did so and the Walshes went out and accompanied the officers to the Police station where they accounted for the ring. Both went out again and proceeded towards Rank-street. There they were seen to enter " The Bird in Hand," and " The Royal Arms," and at each place they were promptly refused refreshment. On coming out of the latter place they created a disturbance and were locked up. The woman became insensible from the effects of drink and had to be medically attended. Both defendants were fined the following day and Inspector Cox visited Mrs. Green and asked if she had any explanation to give for the offence committed and she said she did not notice the condition of the Walshes. She did not deny that ale was served to them. He (the Chief Constable) stated that he did not bring the case ferward as a bad case. It was xot as if the two persons had been in the publichouse sometime and by continuous drinking rendered themselves in a bestial condition. There was however evidence that the law had been broken and he thought it a proper case to be dealt with by the magis- trates.

Sergeant Smith supported the Chief Constable's state- ment.

Mr. Browne did not deny that the law had been broken but said that his client was in total ignorance of the two Walshes previous to their visit to tne Snakespeare. When they cime in it was just going dusk and the police were some 10 minutes in arriving. The defendant had not the opportunity of seeing the condition which she afterwards found her customers were in, to her surprise. They went in and sat down quietly and civilly and there was nothing to show that they were not proper people to be served with a glass of beer. She had only 31. from them and was not therefore, making any profit by per- mitting dunkenness. Mr. Browne considered it a trivial offence and suggested a nominal penalty only. His client he added was a widow with four young children and this was her first offence.

The magistrates retired for a few moments and on re- turning' into court.

_ _ The Mayor said they had deci4ed to fine the defendant 40s. and costs, the licence not to be endorsed. Mr. Browne.-40s. and costs! Councillor Pierpoint.—Yes, 403. and costs. Don't you like it, Mr. Browne ? Mr. Browne.—Well, it was such a trivial case, but of course I can say nothing.

GENEROUS GIFT TO THE PARISH CHURCH. The Warrington Parish Church Magazine" for February has the following :—" A noble gift of £lOO was made last month by a dear friend to the Rec- tor, who was empowered to use it in any way he thought best. As the deficit on the offertory in 1894 was ..E2GO and the prospects of a favourable balance sheet at the end of 1895 did not seem very bright the Rector thought he could not do better than hand it over to the Churchwardens in part payment of their liabilities for the year, which he acc9rdingly did,and it will be found to be accounted for in the yearly statement herewith printed. We cannot thank too gratefully the kind donor for this timely and munificent gift, and quite as much for the loving thought as for the truly generous act."' 9 We understand that the sum of £.200 has been re- ceived from the National Society in aid of the Ifunds of Fairfield Schools.

Speech from the Throne that the origin and cir- cumstances-of;the famous" raid" will form the subject of a searching inquiry and President KRUGER is also reminded of the necessity of re- dressing the "legitimate grievances" of the Outlanders. Turning to what may be called the "domestic "portion of the Speech it is soon seen that no legislation of either a startling or unex- pected nature is suggestea. An attempt is to be made to relieve agricultural distress, though no limit is given as to the form in which the relief will be administered. The elementary schools under voluntary management are described as a "valuable portion of our educational system "and further assistance from public sources is promised. The industrial portion of the community will be interested to hear that an employers liability bill —no doubt allowing plenty of scope for" con- tracting out"—is to be laid before Parliament, and that the delicate subjects involved in the "settlement of trade disputes" are to be tackled.

A SOUTH AFRICA Blue-book which South Africa. was issued on Wednesday throws more light upon the recent course of events in the Transvaal. It appears from the information here given that on December 29, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, who was at his residence at Bir- mingham had his suspicions that an invasion of the Transvaal was contemplated and he tele- graphed to Sir HERCULES ROBINSON, the High Commissioner in South Africa, that if it took place he would have to take action with regard to the South Africa Company's charter. The next day he got a message stating that JAMESON was already on the move. The promptest action was I thereupon taken to disavow the raiders aid to en- deavour to recall them. But JAMESON had to use the language of Mr. RHODES "taken the bit in his mouth and bolted off" with two Maxims, two seven pounder guns, and between five and six hundred troopers, and refused to obey any of the orders to retire. The telegraph wires having been cut a horseman mounted on the best steed that could be got hold of was despatched after the "Scottish knight-errant," with a peremptory order to retrace his stops,but even after this had been de- livered the troopers "saddled up" and proceeded in the direction of Johannesberg. Upon receiving a report of these reftsals, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN bluntly declared that JAMREON "must be mad." There seems to have been in the first instance some reluctance on the part of the directors of the Chartered Company to disavow JAMESON, but on being peremptorily requested by Mr. CHAMBERLAIN 'to either accept or repudiate responsibility for the invasion they chose the latter alternative. Then follows an account from South African officials of the defeat and capture of the invading party and of the subsequent negotiations at Pretoria. It is evident from this Blue-book that President KRUGER at first3believeel that the invasion was countenanced by the Imperial Government and that he thereupon asked for that intervention by the German and Frence Governments which very nearly brought al)out serious results. On the question of suzerainty Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is, how- ever, emphatic and unyielding enough to please the most pronounced "jingo." He approves of the High Commissioner's refusal to even discuss any proposal for the modification of the Conven- tion of 1884 on this point. On the 13th of Janu- ary ho telegraphed to the effect that Great Britain would "resist at all costs the interference of any foreign power in the a Tairs of the South African Republic," and he mentioned at the same time the Government had commissioned "a Flying Squadron of powerful-men-of-war, with twelve torpedo ships, and many other vessels are held in reserve."

Longford Depot has been very little heard of of late, and it is quite a shock to hear of the Warring- ton Rural District Council again taking action by appointing a deputation to wait upon the Warring- ton Corporation to request them to abate the nuisance. The smell, it was stated, was abominable, so abominable that it could be tasted. Mr. Knowles said that he could smell it to his house which is fully two miles away, and he complained that the nuisance was ruining the country around owing to the fact that none would build in that direction. What a strange eventful history Longford depot has had it is undoubtedly one of the institutions of Warrington, yet it is wonderful how many people speak disrespectfully about it.

The" amateur casual" is getting very commo n a the present time, and being so common, he does not excite so much interest as he used to do Frederick Greenwood was the first of the species, and he caused a tremendous sensation when he wrote his series of articles under the title of "The amateur casual," in which he described his ex- periences in Unions in various parts of the country whither he travelled disguised as an ordinary tramp. The example thus set has very often been copied since,but not with much success, and among. the latest to do this sort of thing has been the master of one Union who went, :aceompan ied by a member of his Board, to visit a neighbouring Union to see why the tramps " fought shy" of thst par- ticular Workhouse. Now, if Mr. Billinge, a mem- ber of the Warrington Board of Guardians, is rightly informed, some ef these " Amateurs " have been at the Warrington Workhouse, and have undergone all the treatment awarded to tramps. Whether they are members of the press, or spies from another Union,or have simply posed as tramps out of curiosity is not known, but perhaps we shall soon'hear something complimentary or other- wise about the methods adopted at the local work- house.

THE PANTOMIME AT ST. HELENS.

" ROBINSON CRUSOE."

The production of the pantomime of " Robinson Crusoe " by the famous Leopoldo at the Theatre Royal, St. Helens, has been a great success, and large houses have assembled nightly to witness the performances. The general verdict has been that the pantomime is one of the best ever produced in the town. There is not a dull moment from start to finish—the audience either roaring with laughter or being enthusiastic over capital singing, excellent dancing, or the really clever acrobatic feats of the Leopolds. In the pantomime the old story is followed in the main, and the situations are made as funny as possible. There are some capital scenes,' and in the Palace scene there is a display of splendour, the dresses being more conspicuous than usual for their elegance and pretty design. The Tonnere troupe of dancing contortionists are decidedly clever and deserv- edly encored. The principal attraction is, of courae,the Leopoldo, and the enthusiasm of the audience by the suceess they achieve in a series of difficult acrobatic feats is remarkable, and they are encored time after time. The Boston Twins introduce their specialities and create a good deal of amusement. The transformation scene is entitled "The fairy floral dell," and is prettily arranged, while the Leopoldo are responsible for the harlequinade, which is brimful of fun. We would re- mind our readers that the pantomime will be again pro- duced next week, full particulars of which appear in an advertisement.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, February 15th, 1896.

CONSIDERING the importance of Parliament. some recent events relating to the

foreign affairs of the country, the opening of Parliament on Tuesday was marked by a singular lack of excitement. It was evident that, in the opinion of members of Parliament at all events, the questions which have been raised in one forra or another during the last two or three months, have either passed or have not yet reached the really critical stage. In accordance with the usual custom the QUEEN'S Speech con- tained references to the subjects which have engaged the attention of the Government as well as an enumeration of certain legislative proposals which it is intended to submit to Parliament. The language in which the foreign questions which aro still pending are dealt with is, as might be exnecterl, of a strictly guarded character. No new light is thrown upon the Venezuelan dispute, but the allusions to it are framed in a distinctly conciliatory tone. "The Government of the United States has," says her MAJESTY in the official language of the speech, " expressed a wish to co-operate in terminating differences which have existed for many years between my Government and the Republic of Vene- zuela upon the boundary between that country andmy colony of British Guiana. I have ex- pressed my sympathy with the desire to come to an equitable arrangement, and trust that further negotiations will lead to a satisfactory settlement:' This polite way of dealing with the unexpected intervention of President CLEVELAND has, it is said. made an excellent impression in the -United States. That impression will not have been spoiled by anything that has been said by the leaders of either party in the debate on the address. In the 'House of Lords, Lord ROSEDERY welcomed the action of the United States as it would result in placing a strong and stable government in the place of the weak and unstable administration of Venezuela. In the House of Commons, Sir WILLIAM HAncounT,after enlarging upon the com- plexity of the question, pronounced strongly in favour of arbitration. The country will agree with his declaration that " no false pride or dip- lomatic punctilio" should be allowed to stand in the way of an honourable settlement. The dis- pute has dragged on for fifty years or raore in the hands of dilatory diplomatists and it should now be disposed of once for:all. To leave this open sore to breed further between the two great English-speaking nations would be fatuity of he most reprehensible kind. Lord SALISBURY while stating that no arrangement had yet been arrived at expressed the belief that by a mixed proress of negotiation and arbitration a satisfac- tory agreement would be reached. What is needed on both sides is the exercise of common sense and a sincere desire for peace. On the one hand it is too much to ask that this country should consent to hand over many thousands of British subjects to Venezuela at the will of an arbitrator,and on the other hand there should be a complete and candid recognition of the reasonableness ot the Monroe doctrine from the American point of view, and a withdrawal of all limitations as to the territory— apart from population—that may be submitted to arbitration. The references in the QUEEN'S Speech to Armenian affairs are, as might be expected after the rather humiliating part played by Lord SALISBURY, of the briefest description. It is stated that the SULTAN has sanctioned the "principal reforms ". which have been pressed upon him by the governments of Great Britain, Russia and France, and the fanatical outbreaks of the Turkish Population are " deeply regretted." This seems to be an accurate summary of the PRIME MINISTER'S achievements for the protection of the Christians of Turkey. As to the trouble in the Transvaal the country is again informed in the

SERIOUS SCALDING CASE AT RUNCORN. Joseph Jackson, aged three years,son of John Jackson, of 56, Surrey-st., sustained serious injuries by scalding on Friday. In front of the fire was a guard, and whiltil the little fellow was alone in the house,he leaned over it, overbalanced himself, and in falling upset the teapot. Its contents ran over his face, neck, and chest, which were badly scalded. Dr. McKenzie was called in, and the injuries are of a somewhat serious character. SALT EXPORTS BROM RUNCORN AND WESTON POINT. The shipments of salt from Runcorn and Weston Pohat during January show a decrease of 1,486 tons from Run- corn and the Ship Canal as compared with the corres- ponding period during 1895, and an increase of 393 tons from Weston Point when contrasted with the amount ex- ported during the same period last year. BRUNSWICK MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT CLASS.

At the meeting of this class en Wednesday evening, Captain Le Couteur discoursed very ably on the "Channel Islands," dealing chiefly with Guernsey, of which island he was a native. He spoke of its political history, and its relation to Great Britain. Proceeding, he referred to its beautiful scenery, the fruitfulness of the land, and the people, among whom there were no paupers. The Chairman (Mr. Simister), and Mr. John Bisbrown expressed their appreciation of Captain Le Couteur's services.

THE OPIUM TRADE.

At Greenway-road Sunday school on Tuesday,the Rev. G. A. Wilson, organising secretary of the Society for the Suppression of the Opium Trade, delivered a lecture entitled "Poppies and Pigtails." It was illustrated by a number of lantern views showing the methods of manufacture,distribution,and the enforcement of opium upon China. There was a good attendance, the chair being taken by Mr. J. Wilson. At the close a vote of thanks was tendered the lecturer and chairman. RUNCORN TRADES COUNCIL AND THE PROPOSED GRAVING DOCK. A meeting of the Runcorn Trades Council was held on Wednesday evening at the Brunner Guild Hall, when the following trades were represented :—Shipwrights,water- mrn, engine drivers, joiners, boilermakers, brick-setters and painters. The chief business before the meeting was the consideration of the proposals of Mr. Branch to convert the basin at Weston Point into a dry dock for the repair of vessels. Ihe proposal was most favourably commented upon. and it was unanimously decided to memorialise the Weaver Trustees, praying them to carry out the project. THE PROPOSED TAX ON CYCLES.

At the meeting of the Cheshire County Council on Thursday, the resolutions from the Runcorn Rural and other Councils in the county were read, in favour of the imposition of an annual tax upon cycles, the proceeds thereof to be applied towards the maintenance of high- ways and other purposes, and also the registratißn of cycles.—On the motion ot Mr. Pedley, seconded by Mr. Philip Speakman (Runcorn), a resolution was unani- mously greed to that the taxation of cyclists would be both unjust and undesirable. THE VACCINATION QIIESTiON.

Sveral Runcornians journeyed to Liverpool on Wed- nesday for the purpose of lis:3ning to and taking pars in, a discussion on the" Vaccination Question" with mem- bers of the Pembroke Chapel Literary and Debating Society, Liverpool. The Rev. C. F. Aked presided. The Liverpudlians argued in favour of vaccination, and the opposite views was taken by members of the Brunswick Mutual Improvement Class, Runcorn. The Runcorn representatives were Messrs. Millington, J. D. Jones,and T. Saunders, as well as Mr. J. S, Green, of Warrington. On a vote being taken the anti-vaccinationists lost by a small majority. NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

The Runcorn team journeyed to Broughton on Satur- day and returned one point the better in the combination table. Of course it would have been far more gratifying if it had been two points, but one is better than a cypher, and it has enabled Runcorn to depose Oldham from the 3rd position in the statistical ladder. Some- how or other Runcorn have never fared very well on the Lower Broughton ground,and as the Broughton Rangers are playiqg strongly at the present time, it was not generally expected on Saturday that the wearers of the " myrtle green" would win. Houghton rived a sterl- ing game at full back, but the half back play is des, cribed as selfish in the extreme. This will have to bs remedied if the Runcorn team are to become the happy possessors of the medals, for it cannot be expected that the three-quarters will shine if they do nct receive generous agsistance from the half backs. For the next three Saturdays.Runcorn,who have played more matches away than at home. are to have as thLir visitors the teams from Livarsedge, St. Helens and Manningham, and at the conclusion of these engagements it is anticip- ated that they will have augmented their total points to 42.

HEAVY CLAIM AGAINST A ERODSHAM

FAItME Ct.

On Tuesday, at the Runcorn County Court, before hie Honour Judge Ffoulkes and a jury, Richard Lewis, grazier, of Plas Naqty-Glyn, near Mold, brought an action against flenrrlf. Davies, sheep grazier, of Frod- sham, for £77, being the amount, as alleged, of a ley of sheep on the Wavertree Playground. Liverpool, less £32, which the plaintiff admitted to be due by him to the defendant. There was a counter-claim for £1.4 for money lent. The action had been commenced in the High Court of Justice, but had been sent down to that court for hearing. On behalf of the plaintiff, an agreement was put in, drawn up between the Corporation of Liverpool and the plaintiff, granting to the latter the tenancy of the land for grazing purposes from the 14th Septem- ber last until the 25th March next for £lB—ln cross-examination, the Plaintiff admitted that the defendant accompanied him to Liverpool, when they had an interview with Mr. Henry Herbert, the I superintendent of Parks, at Sefton Park, and that the defendant then paid the rent for the tenancy of the land. Plaintiff also admitted that some time previously the defendant had bought a cow for him, for which he gave £l4, but he (plaintiff) had paid nothing of that money. These two amounts made un the £32 which he now de- ducted from his claim of £77. The defendant sent a number of sheep to the land at Wavertree, and they re- mained there for fourteen weeks. The amount he charged the defendant was 61. per head per week, and 220 sheep grazing on the land for fourteen weeks at the rate of 6. per head per week made up the amount of £77.—The defence was that Davies was the real tenant of the graz- ing land, as he paid the rent and had received a receipt in his name. The counterclaim for £l4 was in respect to the money paid by the defendant for a cow which he bought for the plaintiff.—After a hearing which lasted several hours, the jury returned a verdict for the defen- dant on the claim, and Mr. Greer, who represented the defendant, withdrew his counter-claim.

COUNTY FOOTBALL MATCH AT

RUNCORN.

YORKSHIRE BELIT CHESHIRE.

There was but a moderate" gate "—say 2,000 specta- tors—to witness the County football match at Runcorn on Wednesday, when the Northern Union County teams of Cheshire and Yorkshire faced each other. It will be remembered that the Cheshire representatives won the first engagement with Yorkshire at Headingley, but it was confidently anticipated in York3h;re quarters that the previous result would be reversed on Wednesday. The teams were as follows :

Cheshire—F. Saville (Stockport), back; J. Worsley I and P. Sands (Stockport), H. Myers and T. Warder ! (Runcorn). three quarter backs; Norris (Stockport), J. I Faulkner (Runcorn), half-backs ; A. Garside, T.Gitison, C. Wrigley, J. Roberts (Stockport), W. Faulkner, H. Farmer, J. Langley, n. Walker (Runcorn), forwards. Yorkshire—E. Abbey (Brighouse), back; F. W. Cooper (Bradford), L. Brooke (Brighouse), centre:three-quarters; W. :Hannah (Hunslet), and F. Firth (Halifax), wing three-quarter backs ; R. Sunderland (Manningham) and R. Wood (Liversedge), captain, half backs; M. Sutcliffe (Huddersfield), F. Clegg (Manninghana), W. Walton (Wakefield), 0. Walsh (Hunslet), L. Donkin (Hull), T. H. Hughes (Brighouse), J. Riley (Halifax), and R. Greig (Leeds), forwards. It will be seen that there were important changes in both teams. Houghton and Robinson (Runcorn) were absent from the Cheshire ranks, their places being filled by Saville and Norris, of Stockport, and Myers, of Run- corn, was drafted in to fill Saville's place. Yorkshire were short of Lorimer and Rigg, their respective posi- tions being taken by Abbey. of Brighouse, and Sunder- land, of Manningham. The Turf was in good condition, with a strong breeze blowing from goal to goal. The home county had the advantage of the wind, and Wood started for Yorkshire. Desultory play in the centre marked the opening stages. Warder put Cheshire on the attack with a huge punt, and Wrigley dropped on the ball when a yard short of the line—hard lines. The Yorkshire forwards carried several scrimmages in grand style, but Cheshire returned to ;the attack, Sun- derland stopping a forward rush in marvellous fashion. Afterwards, Cooper getting hold, made a brilliant run, and scored, also placing a goal. Yorkshire now held the upper hand, carrying tha scrimmages in grand style. A long kick by Myers forced a touchdown, and a few minutes later W. Faulkner scored a try, after tscrambl- ing play. Sands made a good run,being well tackled by Abbey. The Ycrkshire threequarters combined splen- didly, and a grand passing run, three-parts the length of the field took place,but was rendered of no avail through Wood passing wildly. A similar fate befel another bout Hannah being the culprit. Wood receiving the ball in the Cheshire 25, dodged over finely, the goal kick fail. jog. At half time Yorkshire were leading by a goal and a try ta one try. Favoured by the wind in the second half, Yorkshire at once invaded the Cheshire quartersiand Sutcliffe dashed over the line, but was thrown into touch-in-goal. War- der, who had been playing well, retired hurt—a great , loss to Cheshire. The home county was hard pressed, ' and Brooke and Firth had bad luck several times, but the latter's persistency was at length rewarded by gett- ing over with two Cheshire men upon him, after a dodging run. Cooper added the goal. Immediately afterwards, receiving the ball from Cooper, Firth easily eluded Saville, and sprinted over the line, Cooper failing this time to place a goal. Cheshire now woke up, and the Yorkshire goal had several narrow escapes, the brothers Faulkner, Norris, and Myers being conspicuous by clever work, and within a few minutes of time Wors- ley was given a chance by J. Faulkner, and took it, to the delight of the spectators. The game ended : Yorkshire, 2 goals, 2 tries; Cheshire, 2 tries. Yorkshire have thus revenged their previous defeat by the Cestrians, and there is no disputing the fact that they well deserved their victory. The team placed in the field by the Tykes was a particularly strong one,and it was difficult to find a weak spot in it. The forwards were far and away the heavier lot, and their play in some measure accounts for their success. Of the three- quarter line Cooper and Firth were the stars,and their fine runs were a treat to witness. Of the home county it must be stated that they were very unfortunate in losing the services of Warder for the latter half of the game, his loss being keenly felt. Another disadvantage was the playing of Saville at full back in place of Hough- ton,who has not yet recovered from injuries he received in the Broughton Rangers match on Saturday. The players and officials were entertained to dinner at _ _

Wilson's Hotel after the match. Mr. P. Speakman, J.P.,

, C.C., presided, and a number of toasts were d,runk. That of the" Queen" was proposed by tbo President, and duly honoured.—Mr. Carroll proposed "the York- shire Team, and congratulated them up their victory. He said the Runcorn eub had benefitted considerably by the formation of the Northern Union as their merits were

now more generally recognised. He believed that the jetsam and flotsam would be attracted to the Northern Uaion, and one great thing in favour of the Union was that its legislation took note of the claims of players to consideration.—Mr. Waller responded, and said he was convinced that the fight had been won by the Northern Union, and that they had carved out a path for them- selves which would be successfully trod. They were prepared to admit any Rugby club into the Union on terms which any Englishman could accept. (Applause.) He appealed to players to play the best football, and to spectators to act like sportsmen. (Applause.) He con- cluded by proposing the the toast of " The Cheshire Team." Mr. J. E. Langshaw, secretary of the Runcorn club, responded. The Yorkshiremen then departed to catch the 6.8 p.m. train for home.

THE ALLEDGED ATTEMPTED MURDER AND SINCIDE AT RUNCORN.

FURTHER POLICE PROCEEDINGS.

Catherine Gregory, a married woman of Penketh-lane, Runcorn, who, it is stated, is of German birth, was on Thursday,again brought before the magistrates at Run- corn Police Court on remand, charged with attempting to murder her child, Gertrude, aged to years, by jumping into the Ship Canal with it at Runcorn on the 3rd February, and also with attempting to commit suicide. The magistrates on the Bench were Mr. James Handley (chairman), and Mr. E. Imison. The prisoner seemed very nervous when in the box. In answer to questions by the Assist- ant Clerk (Mr. Johnson) she said she felt better than on the previous week.:--Questioned by the Chairman she said she had been in this country about twenty years. —Mr. Johnson detailed the evidence given by John Ball, bargeman on he Ship Canal, he being the first to see the woman on the. steps of the lock and afterwards in the canal,and published in our last issue. The witness was not present that morning. The woman had been in Knutsford prison since last Thursday. The Medical Offi- cer at the prison had sent the following report :—" This woman has been under observation since admission. She is dull and depressed, but quite sane.''—The Chair- man said that owing to the absence of a witness, the prisoner would be detainei until the following day, when she would again be brought up. This (Friday) morning, Gregory was committed to Cheshire Quarter Sessions on the charge of attempted suicide.

...._,•__ LOST IN THE DARKNESS AT HELSBY.

FATAL JUMP INTO A QUARRY.

An inquest was held at Helsby, Frodsham, by Mr. H. Churton, on Monday into the circumstances attending the death of Arthur Green. of Godley, near Stockport, and emoloyed as goods guard on the Cheshire Lines Rail. way. From the evidence it appeared ant on Friday, Green went on duty to Helsby, and while there he went for a walk on the hills with a friend named Josiah Lan- cashire. On making their return journey to the station they became perplexed as to the way. They knew little of their surmundings on the hill, and the darkness in- creased their difficulties. On the way a gate had to be climbed,and the two travt-llers at last hit upon what they thought to be the right one. Green climbed over it and gave a leap as he thpught, into the next field, but, to the horror of his companion, he disappeared altogether from sight, and it was discovered he had jumped into a large stone quarry, fully 90 feet deep. Aid was procured as soon as possible, and Green was found un- conscious at the bottom of the quarry. He was attended to by Dr. Byrant who advised his despatch to the Chester Infirmary, but Green died on the way. A verdict of "Accidental Death" was returned.

TAXING COLD.

A" Cold " is not necessarily the result of a high or ow temperature. A person may go from a hot bath directly into a cold one, or into snow even, and not take cold. He may remain out in the coldest atmosphere until chilled through, and still not take cold. On the contrarythe may take cold by pouring a couple of table- spoonfuls of water upon some parts of his dress, or by standing in a door, or before a stove, or by sitting near a window or other opening, where one part of the body is colder than another part. Let it be kept in mind that uniformity of temperature over the whole body is the great thing to be looked after. It is the unequal heat upon ainerent parts of the body that produces Colds, by ' disturbing the uniform circulation of the blood, which in turn induces congestion of some part. Keep the feet always dry and warm, avoid draughts of air, keep the system braced up by plenty of sleep and good food, and by a powerful Tonic, and you will be proof against a Cold and its results. The very best Medicine, either for reventing or curing a Cold, is

MINOR CASES. WARRINGTON BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

when Mrs. Ryder went towards him he dropped the hen and I went away at a rapid rate. P.C. Heaton gave evidence as to arresting the prisoner.—The prisoner denied that he in- tended to steal the hen. He said that he was walking past a gate in Long-lane, where Mrs. Ryder lived, and saw the hen fast in the gate. He went to it and got it out, and just as he had got the hen loose, Mrs. Ryder came up and threw her shovel at him. He then dropped the hen and went away. The magistrates did not apparently believe this story, and fined the prisoner ss. and costs or seven .days' imprisonment In default. BREAKING OPEN A SCHOOLMASTER'S DESK.—Joseph Richardson. a boy about 12 years of age, was charged with breaking open a desk at Penketh Wesleyan Day School, and taking out 55., the property of James Watson, on February 6th. Mr. Watson, the headmaster at Penketh school, said that he left the school on the day in question at about a quarter past five, and had locked up the desk in the scnool- room. In that desk ther3 was a cash box containing about ss. At about eight o'clock the next morning he found the desk lid open and the cash box gone. Th 3 box was after- wards found but the money was musing. From what he was told he questioned the defendant who at first denied it, and ha then gave informatlon to the police.—James Richard- sod, thefather of the boy, said that on Thursday night his eon brought home 3s. 9d., which he said that he had earned. and witness believing him took the money. On the next day witness heard that his son had broken open the desk in Penketh school, and he then handed the money to P.C. Thorley.—P.C. Thorley said that he apprehended the prisoner at his fathers house and charged him with stealing the money, and he admitted having done so.—The magistrates decided to order the boy to come up for judgment when called upon.

Witness requested him to go away, when he took off his waistcoat and. butted him (Clarke) in the breast, knocking him half .way across the street. Witness closed with the prisoner and they both fell to the ground. The man Potter came to witness's assistance, and held prisoner's legs, but it was with grest difficulty that he was locked up.—lsaac Potter gave corrobora tive evidence.—A fine of 55., including costs,fordrunkenness, and 10s. and costs for the assault, was imposed. In default of payment three weeks' imprisonment with hard labour. THREATENING A WM.—John Edwards, of 80. Cockhedge- lane, was charged on warrant with having lased threats to- wards his wife on the 4th inst.—Complainant stated that her husband had not given her 20s. during the past six months. He came home to his meals, and often challenged his own son to fight.—The Chief Constable said this. was prisoner's 30th appearance, and added that he was a dangerous man.—The bench ultimately bound prisoner over for six months, in two sureties of £lO and £2O respectively, bat rot being able to find the necessary sureties, he was sent to gaol for six months. A STUBBORN YOUTH.—Bernard Hettnigan, Pereival's-fold, was charged with having stolen a sovereign. the cash of Levi Touthwaite. on the Bth February.—The Chief Constable stated that the prisoner was tried at that court during the previous week for loitering and obstructing the public foot- path. The case was then adjourned for one month on prisoner promising to leave the town. It appeared that on the Satur- day Mr. Levi Laithwaite, auctioneer, who occupied a stall in the Market-place, engaged prisoner to help him for the night. He asked him among other things to get change for a sove- reign. Prisoner left the stall and did not return. Informa- tion was given to Detective-Sergeant Monaghan, who subse- quently caused him to be arrested at Leigh, when he had 18s. in his possession. When charged with the offence pri- soner said, "I was under a promise to the court to leave the town, and I was not goinglwithout a little money in my pocket."—The Bench taking into consideration the fact of his breaktng the promise which secured him his dircharge a week ago, sent prisoner to gaol for three months with hard lalseur. FURIOUS DatviNG.—John Hamblett marine store dealer, living at 110, Orford-lane, was charged having furiously driven a horse and trap in St. James'st., Latch'ord, on the 23rd January.—The son of the defendant appeared on behalf of his father.—P.C. Cooner deposed that at about eight o'clock p.m. on the 23rd ult. the defendant drove a horse and trap at the rate of from twelve to fo•rrteen miles in hour along St. James'-st. towards Warrington. He was then thrashing the animal and seemed to be the worse for liquor. Witness followed him to the White Hart. public-house, where he (de- fendant) was being refused drink when witness arrived.—The offence being admitted. the Bench fined defendant 10s. and costs. FooTnALL en Route .—A youth named William Taylor, of 34, James-street, and employed as a carter, was summoned for causing a horse and cart to stand for a longer time than necessary in Wilson Patten•street, on the 13th ult —P.C. Clough stated that at 4.20 p.m. he saw the horse and cart near the Old Barracks, no one being in charge. Ten minutes after- wards the horse and cart were still in the same place and alone. Witness then went in search of the defendant, and at last found him in a neighbouring field playing football with about 20 other men. ANOTHER AMERICAN

MESSAGE.

ENGLISHMEN SAID TO BE DISTURBED. CAN IT CAN CAUSE WAR? NEW / ORE, February 14th.—It is reported here that great indignation has been aroused in many parts of England by the actions of certain Americans residing principally in London. Conditions have evidently under- gone a m.irkeci change, for it is stated that whereas the Americans were excited and the English cool after the issuing of the famous message of December last by the American President, on this occasion the Americans are quite undisturbed and the English men and even women are both excited and indignant. The latter accuse the former of deception, oft repeated, and of an aggravating character. We hasten to add, however, that not a few Englishmen side with the Americans, while other; regard the whole matter with interest and even amusement,and say that this difficulty will blow over quite as certainly as did the last. The Press, including many influential papers, is involved; indeed,i is through the medium of the Press that the whole difficulty has arisen. That feeling runs very high is undoubtedly proven by the fact that several newspaper proprietors have been instructed by numbers of their regular subscribers to discontinue sending them papers. Briefly, the difficulty is this: These Americans have introduced into England the "Reading Notice" style of advertisement. Englishmen who are unaccustomed to this style, are thus inducei to read advertisements when they wish to be reading the news of the day, the "Heading N3tice " advertisements and the news being so much alike in style that it is very difficult to distin- guish them, at least until one has read quite halfway through. While some Englishmen say they are amused, and others that they have, through being thus deceived, found remelies which they sorely needed, others declare that it is groes deception, and become so furious that more than one paper is stated to have been destroyed through containing" Reading Notice" advertisements. The above news has so interested us that we have pre- faced our advertisement with it, and propose making use of it in future to help us to make known the fact that Vaccination Virus Vanquisher, or V.V.V., is the only remedy which has ever been discovered that positively prevents the contraction of disease through vaccination, and also cures completely all diseases which have been contracted by vaccination in the past. It has cured already large numbers of the most chronic and obstinate cases of diseases of the blood, skin, and internal organs, which diseases have been contracted through vaccina- tion. Being antiseptic in action and vegetable in origin it quickly destroys the germs of these diseases and heals the injury that has been done, and thus clears and purifies both blood and tissues which have been polluted and injured through vaccination. No mother should allow one of her children to be vaccinated without using this splendid safeguard. Every Chemist sells it at 2s. 9d. per bottle, and one bottle is sufficient to protect a child through the whole course cf its vaccination, several bottles being required to cure chronic old- standing diseases. It is also sold by Barclay and Sons. 95. Earrington-street, London, 8.C., and by the V.V.V. Company, Limited, 31, Snow Hill, London, E.C.

DIE EFFECTS OF MODERN RIFLE

BULLETS.

Dr. Delorme, surgeon-in-chief of the French Army ,has been lecturing before the Paris Academy of Medicine on the new steel-coated rifle bullets and their effects as pro• jected from the modern magazine rifle. The most re- markable fact in connection with them appears (says the" Daily News" correspondent\ to be this, that they cause very little pain fo those who are struck by them. At Fourmies, during the riots there, one man was wounded so badly that he afterwards suffered from par- alysis ; yet he did not even susnect that he was shot until he saw the bloodstains upon his cloths. Anothcr man, who was shot through the leg, described his sen- sations thus t--" I felt a slight shiver come over me." Another, who was shot through thr arm, could only re- member that his elbow twitched, and that involuntarily he closed his fist. Dr. !Delorme finds that when the bullets meet with an obstacle at a short distance, say from 100 to 150 yards,they are very apt to explode,and the pieces of metal are capable of doing serious mischief.

WARRINGTON v. HUDDERSFIELD. .A crowd of about 4,000 spectators assembled at Wil- derspool on Saturday to witness the first match between these two teams. The day was a very unfavourable one, and under ordinary circumstances, there would pro- bably have been only a small "gate," but the re-appear- . _ .

ance of " Smack" Bate in the first team from which he had been absent since the match at Bradford on the third Saturday in September,had aroused great interest in the town, and there was a general desire to see how he

would play after his long rest. His :play during the match was of a very high order, and new that he is a

regular member again, the team will no doubt be very much stronger. Huddersfield are unfortunate enough to

be at the bottom of the League, but this is dae more to bad luck than anything else, and it has only been through lack of finish that they have such a poqr record. They almost invariably give a good game. but are poor scorers. This being taken into consideration, the match was not such an easy thing as proper form would show, and the match on Saturday was a very stiff affair in- deed, Warrington beicg exceedingly fortunate in not being scored against. Mr.Boydell, of Leigh, was the re- feree, and the following were the players comprieing the two opposing teams : Warrington : Heesom, full back ; Barber, Barton, Cross, and Breadv, threequa.rters ; Bate and :Foden, ; halves ; Nevins,Burrows, Barrett, Hill, Saunders,Taylor,

Fairhurst, and Swift, forwards. Huddersfield : France, full back: Boothroyd,Forsyth, Smith, and Wimpenny, threequarters ; Wood and Old- field, halves ; Garside, Sutliffe, Connolly, Littlewood, Lockwcod, Wilson, Stevenson, and Lodge. forwards. Barber. on winning the toss, took advantage of a stiff breeze which was blowing towards the railway goal,a.nd Ledge kicked off for the visitors,and the first scrimmage took place in Warrington ground, this being supple- mented by a good round of passing between Bur- ton, Barber, and Cross, but the latter's pass to Bate was a little forward. Smith gained the ball from a scrimmage, and passing to Boothroyd. the sprin- ter of the Huddersfield team went off at a great rate.but Broady managed to haul him down at the quarter flag. Burton was at length instrumental in clearing his lines by a long kick in touch, and Taylor also got the ball into still safer quarters. A. grand dribble taken part in by , the visiting forwards regained all the lost advantage, and things commenced to look ominous for Warrineton when Ileesom got the ball and kicked to centre. Had- der.seeld,despite the adverse wind,returned to the attack with commendable pertinacity. and were again in full cry for the goal line, when Broady made his mail under great difficulties, and by one of his ponderous kick found touch again at the centre line. Here " Smack " B ite got away nicely, and passed to Burton who very narrowly missed dropping a goal, but only a minor was the result. After the kick out, a good dribble was taken part in by Burrows and Broady and the ball was worked over the line, but it unfortunately crossed the dead ball line before Broady could capture it. Huddersfield now commenced to play up again, and a most dangerous dribble took play to the oppesite end, and Sutcliffe had a clear run in, taut the pass from Garside to him bad been forward, and thus the chance was lost. Play now like a pendulum swung back to the opposite end of the field and touch was found a little beyond the Tykes' quarter flag, and a line out took place. Bate astutely threw to Taylor who was rot " marked " sufficiently, and the latter was enabled to rush over the line near the corner flag just before France and Bnothroyd came upoe the scenc. rho place was a very difficult one, but Tay- lor made a very good, though unsuccessful attempt to get a goal. Soon a,ftsr the restart, Bate passed to Bur- ton and the latter to Broady, who only missed dropping a goal by the merest shade. Huddersfield again bore down oe the Warrington stronghold. cnlv to find the Warrington defenders in rare form, and Warrington as quickly returned the compliment by taking the play to the opposite end where Swift had an unsuccessful kick at goal from a penalty and they were busily attacking when half time was called with Warrington leading by a try to nil. With only a try to the good, and having a strong breeze to face, Warrington were in anything but a safe position, and the result was a most open one. Hill's kick off was blown back, and had to be taken a second time, and this time, France failed to return the ball, and a scrimmage took place right on the Tykes' lines. Do 1 what they could, Warrington could not score and Booth- I royd at length cleared the danger. A fine run by I Connolly was finished by a pass to Sutcliffe, and the latter actually got over, but was called back owing to the fact that the pass to him was forward. Several scrim- ' mages took place right under the Warrington goal posts, and it is marvellous however the Hudderafielders were kept out. They were however kept nut, and Cross, Barber, Bates, Foden and Burton were instrumental in clearing the danger, for a time, but Huddersfield again got dangerous, and another scrimmage took place on the line. Foden by a long hick in touch again repelled them and then a dribble by the Warrington forwards took .play to the opposite end, and to make amends for their previeus play in this half, Warrington penned Hudders- field in their lines, and scrimmages took place with a much too close proximity to the Huddersfield line for the liking of the latter. No score came, however, despite the efforts of the local team, and some midfield play was witnessed, Warrington again came with a rush, and Smack Bate made a clever mark right in face of the goal pasts. Foden was entrusted withlithe kick, but be unaccountably failed to take advantage of the easy place, and instead of increasing the lead by four points, only a useless minor was gained. Warrington still con- tinued to press, and Cross had an unsuccessful drop at goal, this making the third attempt during the game which the Warrington players just failed to bring off. Huddersfield now seemed to get their second wind.and a most dangerous run was made by Sutcliffe right through the thick of his opponents, and he had got beyond the Warrington quarter line, when he rushed into the loving embraces of Barber and Cross, who would not allow him to go any further in that direction. Boothroyd bad another attempt to cross the line, but Broady was again a thorn in his side and thus the play continued, first one side having the advantage, and then the other side, neither being able to score, and when the whistle finally sounded, the score stood as at the interval : Warrington one try, Hudaersfield nil. WIDNES- v. TYLDESLEY. The return game between Widnes and Tyldesley was played on the Lowerhouse-lane ground, Widnes. before 3,000 spectators. Widnes played against the wind in the first half, and there being no return to Barber's kick Widnes were at once on the aggressive. Thanks to some good kicking by the Tyldesley centres, the pressure was eased, and in the scrummages which ensued in midfield the Tyldesley forwards used their weight to good advan- taw,, and were instrumental in giving their team a footing in the Widnes "25." The home defence was equally as good as that of the visitors, who were soon forced back to the centre, where a lot of desultory play took place. Widnes attacked for some time,but nothing tangible resulted, a similar fate befalling an attempt by Tylelesley at the other end, and the interval was called without either side having scored. Woodward re-started, and the home team (although several of them were suffering from injuries sustained in the first half) at once made it evident that no stone was to be left unturned to gain the victory. After the lapse of a few minutes Tyldesley were penned in their own " 25,"but for a long time their defence withstood the spirited and determined attack. Then Drummond made a fine but unsuccessful drop at goal, which he followed up by scoring a capital try,after a beautiful round of passing. Brennan'a place kick was a failure. The reverse demoralised the Tyldes- ley team, and their defence fell away to nothing. Drummond obtained possession from the pack, and, after the ball bad passed through the bands of the line of backs, Alford scored try number two, but the place kick was again a failure. There was only one team in it from this point, and it was not hinee before Nevitt still further increased the lead of the home lot by scoring a brilliant try, after hoodwinking three or four of the Tyldesley backs. There was no sting in a final attack by the visitors, and the whistle sounded leaving Widees with a meritorious victory by one <Yoh], two tries (eleven points) to nothing. A con- temporary sacs the surprise to those who witnessed the game is not that the score was sa large, but that it was net,at least, doubled. RUNCORN v. BROUGHTON RANGERS.

The great disparity existing between the Alpha and Omega of the Lancashire section did not prevent between 3,000 and 4,000 spectators turning out at Breuglaton to see them engage In mimic strife. George Faulkner was away from Runcorn, whilst Chapman was unable to assist the Rangers, his place at centre being filled by an uotried novice, named Leadbetter. The game was only in its infancy when Warder ran to the Ranger's line, and though Leadbetter initiated a nice passing run, the 1" myrtle green" warriors were quickly back again, and for fully 20 minutes the Rangers were decidedly over- played. The Runcorn forwards got possession repeatedly and Faulkner and Rebinson had a gay time of it at half, but, fortunately for the home side, they preferred to stick to the leather, and the great efforts the visiting for- wards were making were thrown away. During the closing stages of the first half the Rangers began to get the measure of their opponents,and Messenger, using his feet well, placed Runcorn on the defence. Hard scrum- maging was the order, and, though the Rangers were at this point overplaying Runcorn, Houghton and Warder saved magnificently, and the whistle blew with the leather in the Runcorn line. The players had barely re- sumed when Hall was placed " hors de combat," and for over half an hour the Rangers were a man short. They, however, worked the ball to more congenial quarters, only to see Myers get a free kick from a favourable posi- tion,bat Robinson interposed his burly form betwixt the goal and the kicker. Time was drawing to a close, and no score, wl,en Faulkner passed out to Butterworth,only to see Lead better snap the pass and punt over Houghton's head; Berry was the quickest to the leather, and drib- bled right away from the centre. He succeeded in cros- sing the line first, but Warder charged him over, and Butterworth kicked dead to the great chag,,rin of ; the home supporters. Nothieg further took place of any note, and the game ended in a pointless draw. " Jingle -" 1 in his cogitations on the game says it was of a most interesting nature, and probably a draw is the fittest _

result, though Berry had very hard lines. Runcorn were, however, pressing a great deal, and Faulkner and Robinson got possession so often that a score should have resulted, but they neglected their three-quarters, and the opportunities were let slip. The Rangers,though a man short, played the stronger team at the finish, as the Runcorn forwards were unmistakably blown by their earlier efforts. Hacking played one of his best games at back, whilst Barratt, Berry and Leadbetter were smart at three-quarter, and Messenger and Garrity showed capital form at half. The forwards played well to a man. Houghton had plenty to do, and did it well. The Runcorn three-quarters had to make most of their own play, the only failure being Gayter. Of the forwards Farmer, Hughes and Walker orked hard.

HOUSEHOLD LINENS. hemmed and marked free of charge. 3. and W. Dutton. Sankey-at., Warrington.—.4dvt. LASTERN SERVICE AT THE YMCA. _ .

A new departure was made at the Y.M.C.A. on Sunday in the form of a" lantern" service. When the meeting had been opened with singing and prayer, Mr. W. H. Ireland. gave a fine rendering of the solo " The Star of Bethlehem," which was illus- trated by beautiful limelight views. The next item was the "Life of Christ," with short explanatory re- marks by Mr. T. S. Kent. Some 50 views of this interesting subject were shown, after which Miss Kent gave a sympathetic rendering nf "The better land." These picture illustrations were also very beautiful, and being shown by the powerful lantern of Mr. D. S. Stone (who contributed so much to the enjoyment of the service), were much admired. All seats were free, and the meeting commenced after t the church services had concluded, viz., at eight o'clock. The hearty sining of the hymn "Abide with me" brought a profitable meeting to a close, Mr. F. Hickman played the accompaniment. Mr. William Roberts presided over the P.S.A. weekly meeting, and referred to the unrest and anxiety ex- perienced during the past few weeks, owing to the strained relations between this country and other governments, remarking that in his opinion the Christian influences at work:in our own land as in the United states of America had averted a terri- ble calamity, The speaker for the day was Mr. Rovlance, of Moore. who dwelt upon the various influences of the Y.M.C.A. work, and gave a few words of encouragement to the officials of the P.S.A. in particular. He rejoiced in the evident success of the movement,and advised all the members to prize very highly the privi:eges which were theirs. Mr. Yonge, jun.. was the soloist, and gave a capital rendition of the solo "Thou art passing hence." Messrs. Joseph Critchley and Holford,jun.. officiated 'upon the piano and organ respectively. There was I again a large attendance.

STOCKTON HEATH.

FRIMROSE LEAGUE.—A very successful -meeting of the Appleton Habitation of the Primrose League was held in the schoolroom,Strettou,on Friday evening week. There were present Mrs. Lyon (Ruling Councillor), Miss Lyon and party, the Rev. Canon Dodd, Mrs. Dodd and party, Mrs. Harrison Tinsley, Miss Jackson (Leeds),Dr.Moherly, Sec. Mrs. Lyon opened the proceedings by moving a vote of condolence to her Majesty on the lamented death of the late Prince Henry of Battenberg, and this was carried. Afterwards a lantern lecture, entitled " Land- marks in English History " was given, Mr. Frith mani- pulating the lantern. The Rev. Canon Dodd was the lecturer, and he dealt with the subject in an excellent manner. Patriotic songs were rendered be Sir Reginald Hardy, Dr. Moberley,and Mrs. Harrison Tinsley, whose singing of " Home, Sweet Home" was much admired. An interval took place, during which tea and refresh- ments were served ,the caterer being Mr. James Howard, The second part of the programme consisted of a con- cert, violin and violoncello solos being given by Miss Lyon and Miss Choleiondeley in a masterly manner. Miss Blumberg gave a finished interpretation of Ruben- steins' " Valse Caprice," which was much apprecia'ed. Miss Blumberg was a pupil of the late Sir Charles Hallee and is an accomplished musician. Songs were also given by Mrs. Harrison Tiesley, Mr. Geoffrey Dodd, and Mr. Nlacdoue all contributed a humorous sketch, entitled r The Silver Wedding," which caused roars of laughter. A hearty vote of thanks was passed to the Ruling Coun- einer and to the entertainers. Sir Michael Costa's set- ting of the National Anthem was sung with much spirit I by the audience, Mrs. Lyon and Mr. J. Berry taking the solo parts. A word of praise is due to the Executive Committee for providing such an excellent entertain- ment. ST. THOMAS' Y. M. MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT CLASS.—A meeting in connection with the above class was held in the club-room on Monday evening. There was a large attendance, Mr. Miller being in the chair. Mr. Dallow re-intrauced the subject of "The war scare," dealing principally with the Armenian question, which was well taken up and warmly discussed, so much so that on the vote being taken it was unanimously agreed to bring up the subject at the next meeting. Votes of thanks were passed to the Chairman and to Mr. Dallow. FUNERAL OF P.C. SfIATWELL.—The remains of the late Police-constable Shatwell, who had been stationed at Higher Walton for many years, and who died a few days ago, were on Monday removed by road to Wilmslow for interment. The deceased had been attended by Drs. Adams and Bower. His death is due to a complication of ailments. He was greatly respected, not only by his superior officers and fellow-constables, but by the peoVe of Walton generally. He leaves a widow and three chil- dren to regret his lose. LECTURE ON "THE PASSION ruy."—There was a large audience on Saturday evening in the Sc. Thomas's Church Institute assembled to listen to a lecture by Mr. Arthur Evans on " The Oberammergauers and their Passion Play." Alderman Francomb occupied the chair, and introduced the lecturer in a few fitting remarks.—Mr. Evans treated his subject in an exceedingly able and lucid manner, illus- trating it by more than 200 lime-light pictures by the aid of a powerful lantern. He traced the origin of the play amongst the inhabitants of the quiet little village of Oberammergau, in the Bavarian highlands described the nature of the people and their surroundings, their every-day life before and after the " Passion " season ; and lastly gave his hearers an admirable sketch of the representation itself as seen in 1890, at Oberarnmergau, the last occasion of its production. Everyone of the artistes, explained the lecturer, was a born Oberarniner- gauer. and when not performing was following ordinary pursuits of labour ploughing in the field, carving in the workshop. Sze. About nine months More the repre- sentation—which occurred only once in every ten years —the villagers began to prepare for it, and cultivated their appearance, particularly their hair, so as to as closely resemble the characters they were to impersonate. The young men and women studied their parts, and did their best to live them in fact ; the painters and sculptors were busy, and also those of the inhabitants who had been selected to com- pose the orchestra and chorus. It was a noteworthy fact that not a single wig was worn by such a vast band of players,and that all the materials needed for the pro- per production of the play were prepared by the villag- ers and not by outsiders as many people supposed. As the " Passion" season approached the village assumed a most artistic appearance,thanks to the efforts of the in- habitants and their natural talents for decorative work. The performers had by this time so perfected themselves in their respective roles, that Hot the slightest hitch was feared. Thousands of people from all parts of the earth of all ages—prom 20 to 80 years—flocked into the village to witness the performance,which began at eight o'clock in the morning and lasted until five o'clock in the even- ing,admitting but of an hour and. a quarter for refresh- ment and rest. Some persons visited the place to scoff and carry back material for jokes.but they had not been there long before they were forced to abandon these in- tentions,so impressed were they by the performance. In fact persons of all shades of religious thought had testi- fied,after witnessing the play, that it was both morally and spiritually elevating, and failed not to beneficially affect many of those who saw it. The lecturer,to render his subject more pleasant, introduced many items of the "Passion Play " music,some of which he sung, playing also the accompaniments on the organ; whilst Other portions he entrusted to a small chorus. The delivery of the lecture lasted about two hours, and at its conclu- sion Mr. Evans was the recipient of a hearty vote of thanks.

BURNING FATALITY AT WARRINGTON

...9n Thursday night the death took place of isva Ashcroft,two years of age. who lived with her Parents at No, 1, Navigation-street. It appears that Oil Tuesday morning, the deceased child, with an Older brother, was playing in the kitchen, when the mother had occasion to leave the house. She tad just got into the street when she heard:screams !Ind on returning to the house found the little girl 3.1.2 flames. Dr. McCarthy was called in, but after lingering two days in great pain she succumbed to ber injuries. Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest Yeateiday, on the body, at the Parade room. Sarah Ann Ashcroft, mother of the child said she thought that the child had set herself on fire with a piece Of paper. The jury returned a verdict of Acciden- tal Death.
