Erttcro to the !Mil:or.

MR. PEERS AND HIS MISSIONARY FALLACIES.

Sir,—Continning from my last letter and referring to Mr. Peer's lecture to Christians on the value of their mis- sionary efforts, one can only be struck with his unique qualification for the task, namely, that be knew little or nothing about his subject. Ile seemed to imagine that his knowledge of the Confucian maxim "Do unto others as you would they should do to you" covered every necessity of the case. It was apparently not worth his trouble to bestow a thought as to the evidential influence for good that this maxim had ever exerted upon Chinese character, or upon the progress of that nation from the days of Confucius down to the present time. All very well that some scattered rays of truth should be dis- covered in the literature of many heathen nations. What finer conceptions of moral beauty, for instance, can be met with than on the pages of Greek and Roman drama- tists and moralists? But what did it all amount to? Did the condition of the Roman peoples when Christ appeared in the world demonstrate that nothing further was needed for its well-being but fine and beautiful senti- ments in morals and religion? Is it a fact or not, that an appalling conditon of things, as vile and cruel, as impure and devilish as the imagination can conceive, reigned supreme throughout the whole Roman State at that time? Let Matthew Arnold describe it in a verse: "On that hard pagan world disgust And sacred loathing fell; Deep weariness and sated lust Made human life a hell." . .

To this hell Jesus Christ came, and wih what effect Mr. Peers? He bade His messengers go forth and carry His gospel to all the wretched race. They were not to let fine maxims and passages which the nations could produce from their moralists and philosophers to stand in their way. To a Celsus a Porphyry, or a Julian this might have seemed something like your graceful con- ception of" teaching your grandmothers, v &c., but the "intrusive fanatics" weat on, and did not stop despite three centuries of tortures, cruelties and deaths, until the pagan empire was overturned, and a professed Christian sat on the throne of tl-e civilized world. Let , me ask you Mr. Peers was this not a blessed exchange ' , from the old pagan rule? Despite the fall;ngs short of Christendom, has it not been a mighty gain for mankind at large that the Apos- tate Julian was obliged to confess" The Galilean had conquered " ? Yet,these Missionaries of the Cross went to a people "unwilling to rece've them" and stoutly brushed aside the adversaries wbo would tell them they professed many fine sentiments anwng their own teachers' and that it was 'rather anomalous" to be sending folk to instruct them. Your logic Mr. Peers is equally as bad and worthless. It was not fine maxima merely which were wanted. It was the regenerating' influence of a divine life accompanied by the presence and power of a divine spirit changing the human heart, and so enabling men to exhibit in mani- fest operation, the beautiful sentiments they might have met with in their literature and philos- ophy. Now Mr. Peers would have shown truer wisdom if he had listened more attentively to the teach- ings of the better class of heathen literature. Amongst its authors are some of the men of acutest intellect that ever lived, and who sought earnestly to solve "the Enigmas of life. " We will wait," says Plato, " for One, be it a God, or a God-inspired men to teach us our religious duties, and to take away the darkness from our eyes." "Oh if one only might have a guide to truth," cried Seneca. Such a guide and such a teacher has come; but our modern Solon in the true spirit of the Roman paganism of the first three centuries would have us to believe that the "heathen Chine° " needs none of the light he has thrown upon the Enigmas of this life an.l of the life to come. But I must crave the opportunity Mr. Editor for some further reckonings with Mr. Peers upon this subject.—Yours truly, 6th February, 1896.

Z AMINEH.

===== 0002601_18960215_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
CHRISTIAN "CONTRACTING OM."

"Scepticism is the highest of duties, blind faith the one un- pardonable sin."—Huxley. Sir,—The noble art of "contracting out" is not confined to the commercial world. This rather question- able mode of evading their responsibilities and shuffling out of their obligations is not unknown or unpractised by the Christians. As the man of the world contracts himself out of the Acts of Parliament, so the man so full of" other worldliness " contracts himself out of the Acts of the Apostles. Be is fully alive to the truth that "the letter killeth " when it is confronted with facteacd found to be fiction. To the impartial student of ecclesi- astic history the last five hundred years furnishes ample evidence of both the need and the deed. As might be expected of the traditions of a semi-barbarous race, who let their imaginations run riot, and then imagined their imaginings to be facts ;who were ignorant of the virtues —nay the !duty—of investigations and verification, the " scriptures " are full of statements utterly at variance with known facts. What wonder then that these "documents "—the work of confessedly ignorant men— should require this double shuffle of reconciliation ? And the fact of this need if the "rectification of the frontiers" is the best proof of the human °Hein of these "docu- ments." Apart from the blasphemous impiety of ascribing to the Supreme, thoughts and actions unworthy in a man—such as commanding the murder of innocent women and children, the rape of Midianite maidens, &c.—is it not like a crime to impute to the "All Wise" blunders of man's making and blunders which are now delected and corrected by man? Objecting as I do to the idea of vicarious sacrifice-1 wishing every tub to stand upon its own bottom, if it has one—l most strenuously object to the fathering of our own blundering imbecilities upon " The Eternal '— the "God of Truth." And this is done abundantly in what the ignorant so loudly proclaim as the "Book of god " ! The so-called " Scriptures" are essentially human—human in their physical errors, in their poetry and patbetic narrative and alas, too human, in their obscenities. The verbal revision of these very human writings will shortly have to be followed by a moral revision in which Biblical filth shall be eliminated. When this bit of" contracting out" is effected " Tne Book" will be better worth exporting to India and Cline on the noints of British bayonets; and there might then be less objection to the Book as a vehicle for the conveyance of a rechaufe of Chinese morality of China. The business of "contracting out" has been a brisk one ever since physical investigation took the place of uncritical credulity, and is as brisk as ever. The list of "articles of faith-" out of which Christians have con- tracted themselves, or are now endeavouring to do so, is a long one, but here is a selection of things once held essential to salvation—special creation h. six days ; the age of the world; its astronomical status; the resurrec- tion of the dead, animal or vegetable ; disease, as diabol- ical possession ; witchcraft, &c., &c. And now, lo! "A Layman" wants to contract himself out of Original Sin! But I must ask" Zaminer " to throw the " documents" at his head, and ask if they "have no validity" for him ? And what a figure " Amos White " cuts in his attenspt to contract himself out of the pro-slavery statements of " the scriptures ! The way " Amos "ignored Geneses ix. 25,Exodus xxi.6,Leviticus xxv. 44,and St. Luke xii. 47, showed his want of appre- ciation of the validity of the documents ; and the excess of his zeal over his knowledge of what he was defending. Here is another cpnfirmation of opinion I have long held —that the less we "know" of the "Scriptures " the more zealous we are in our belief in,and defence of them. "Ignorance is the mother of devotion," and the father of the Fidei defensor. But, when all the" contracting out" is done there will be nothing to defend, and then perhapa the modern pulpit—which shoule be the ros- trum for the promotion of the truth, the whole truth, and nothing but the truth,will cease to be the" refuge of lies" and a perpetuator of the ignorance of the centuries. And when the bibliophagous " contrac- tor " has eaten his way through the text and there is nothing left but the binding—then men will see that what man can so easily unmake must be man-made; when perhaps the best interests of mankind may receive the attention now frittered away in devotion to a fantas- tic faith. As I abominate lying—whether for the "greater glory of God" or the advancement of The missionary cause, I would ask " Zaminer " whether the suggestie falsi or the suppressio ten i was uppermost in his mind when he wrote "Who, for instance, are they who attempt to 'force' Christianity upon an unwilling people? There is not a society in existence that does aught of the kind." Note here the suggested lie—no missionary society uses "force," therefore, no force is used ! How long would these "intrusive fanatics" (Chinese estimate, Mr. "Zaminer") remain in Chinese territory if not supported by the " force" of British bayonets supplied by British taxes But, these withdrawn, will Zaminer " have the " effrontery" to maintain that even the maddest missionary would dare to enter the Celestial Empire to carry the "divine" command —" But those mine enemies, which would not that I should reien over them. bring hither, and slay them before me," Luke xix., 27 ? Exit missionary! Now, I am not infatuated with the" heathen Chinee " whose Ways are certainly " peculiar." but I hold him perfectly justified in resisting the intrnsion of these fanatics, and ourselves most blameworthy for "forcing" this nuisance upon them. Divine command! Fiddle sticks! This is a matter of good taste and propriety; and I know of no divine command to make yourself a nuisance to your neighbour. Reverse the case, and see how we should like it. Therefore "Do unto others as you would they should do to you."—Yours JOHN PEERS. February 2nd, 1896.

MR. JOHN PEERS AND "A TALE OF A TUB." "Want due conaistence."—Wordsworth.

Sir,—Mr. John Peers, in his last letters, has proved hin4self to be inconsistent. In his first letter he makes a noise about the" utility and benefit of missionary work" and " teaching your grandmother to suck eggs." In his second letter he says that" no such person as' Jesus Christ' ever lived upon the earth." In his le*Aers of last Saturday's issue he" harks back "to his " missionary apologetics and theological offal," Z-zc. In the first place allow me to say that, as clever as Mr. Peers may be, he must admit that there have been

persons gifted with mere intellectual ability.. I refer to such persons as John Wycliffe, "eminent as a scholars a diplomatist, and a preacher," the first to ti anslate the Bible into English,and who sacrificed worldly possession, for the caese of Christ; John Huss, "one of the bravest of the martyrs who have died in the cause of honesty and freedom, of progress and of growth toward the light." And I may go on to Jerome of Prague, Martin Luther, William Tyndal, Hugh Latimer, Nicholas Ridley, John Bunyan, John Wesley, William Miller.&o., Sze., Sec. The lives of such men will teach Mr. Peers something if he will only study them. Referring to his remarks as tothe "utility and benefit 'of missionary work," and to its being • backed by British bayonets," I should like to ask him if he has ever heard or read of Dr. Joseph Welff, the famous Hebrew missionary and traveller. Did he not travel in the most barbarian countries without the protection of any European authority? and when warned against being unarmed amongst savage and warlike tribes, he declared himself provided with arms—" prayer,zeal for Christ, and confidence in Ills help." Mr. John Peers does not know, perhaps, that" on the islands of the Fiji group, formerly notorious for their cannibalism, bloodshed and cruelty, out of a population of 120,000,there are now considerably more than 100,000 who attend regularly public worship at the various churches, while there are over 42,000 children in attend- ance at the day schools" ; and yet, Mt. Peers has the audacity to say that the alissionary object is a failure. I myself, fail to see it. Further, he says, "no such person as Jesus Christ' ever lived upon this met ! Jesus of Nazareth was a Jew—he admits that there was a Jesus of Nazareth. Mr. John Peers is not certain whether Jesus of Nazareth ever heard the name" Christ" applied to himself. He says that he did not "in all probability." He is, however, most certain that Jesus of Nazareth would not have answered to the Greek appellation. I refer him to my letter of the 11th inst. as to that. Mr. Peers, your authorities were evidently at fault and you, in youself, can do nothing. Allow me to suggest (to you that "It is never too rate to mend." " A man can never truly believe what he has not at some time questioned," it is written. We, undoubtedly, need a revival, and you are just the man to commence one, and ' if you will only put aside "your authorities" and cling to the gosipl of Christ—which cannot be doubted—you will do much good to your fellow creatures. Is not Chrsitianity the first and only religion that teaches for- giveness of sins on the simple ground of repentance? Does Christianity make a man better or worse? The Greeks, I know, impersonated in some fables—do not some of our writers do the same to-day ?—but that only adds to the truth of the Bible. If there were no good coins there would be no counterfeits. The clearest revelation of righteousness comes by Christ. It has been said that "a sound nature, seeking without bias, will see the truth," and if there is a reputed atheist whose heart is true and sound and whose life is righteous, 'say that such an one believes in a Good.in a spiritual centre, however his conception of divine wisdom and love may differ from the popular conception, or the theological dogma which bears that name. In conclusion, allow me to review your letters and give you my opinions : is'. "You say that Missionary Enterprise is a failure." I say that it is not a failure when carried out on purely Christlike lines. (See my aforementioned prcof). You say that 2nd. Jesus Christ did not live upon this earth, but Jesus of Nazareth did. Where is the difference? 3rd. You say, "If this was the tribal God of the Jews —it would be interesting to know what the devil was like." God's ways are mysterious and you cannot judge them. You will pardon me if I quote from Coulson Kernahan's:" God and the ant," but the idea explains my feelings in the matter. "Tan the ant creep up into the brain of man, to see man's world as man sees it? Yet has man.whose whole world is in the eyes of God as one ant in a universe, thought to creep into God's brain, to see as He sees,to think as lie thinks, and to judge the Omnipotent One by man's little laws." 4th. If you cannot believe in Christ or Christianity will you allow me to beg of you not to endeavour to win young people over to your side and way of thinking un- til you can offer and show them how to te better men and women ? It is not an unfair request, and, believe me,Mr. Peers,you yourself, although you have said such contrary things yet you are,in your own heart,ill at ease because of your thoughts. I perceive,that you want to do good but cannot, because you will feel like a hypo- crite. I cannot leelp,after all, feeling an admiration for a man who "speaks cut" his c3nvictions. But, Mr. Peers,stude the life of the Jesus of Nazareth and if you can point out a better example of a true, contrite, holy and upright rnan'please du so.I should like to know such an one. Christ's life shows us that a man can he pure if he likes and live,not for himself ale ne, but for his fellow creatures. Reciprocating your very kind wish for a prosperous New Year, I am, yours truly. VIVIAN GREY. P.S.—Through the kindness of the Editor I have re- ceived a letter from a person in Lymm who says that I should not have written" young men will be privileged to speak out" in my first letter, but "courageous enough." Be that as it may—l have spoken. V. G.

SHIP CANAL.

THE PORT OF MANCHESTER: LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

The traffic upon the Ship Canal last week was some- what above the average, not only in respect of the nam- ber of arrivals and departures, but also as to the volume of merchandise dealt with. As compared with the re- turns for the preceedin,e; seven days the coastwise trade increased by thirty ships, hut the tonnage was less by 3,009 registered tons; whilst in the foreign department there was an increase of two ships and 5,711 tons. The number of vessels navigating the canal without cargo was less than in the previous week. Amongst the arrivals were twenty-two vessels with general cargoes, seventeen with china clay, &e.., and fourteen with scone and slate. Nine steamers entered the canal with pig iron from Middlesbrough, Harrington, Millorn, Myer- ston, Ardrossan, and Barrow-in-Furoess, and two with tinplate bars. Large consignments of green fruit were entered by two steamers from Spain and two from Egypt and Syria. Four steamers arrived with wood-pulp from the Baltic;' and amongst other notable imports , were 7,485 bales of cotton from New OrleansiAlexandria, and New York ; several thousand quarters of grain from New Orleans and Russia, 1,203 tons of sugar from Dante zic, and 3,000 barrels of oil from New York. The ex- ports included twenty-five general cargoes (amongst which were one each to China, the Persian Gulf,and the Brazils), thirteen cargoes of coal, three of salt, two of purple ore, one of pitch, and one of bricks and clay. A new direct service to Lisbon and Oporto was inaugu- rated by the departure of the steamer Fez. CHE3HIRE LANDOWNER 3 AND THE NEW BILL. A meeting of landowners within the district of the Frodsham and Mislay Drainage Board was held in the Town Hall, FrOdsham,on Wednesday, for the purpose of considering the proposals of the Ship Canal Company in their Bill to close the tidal openings,and the effect likely to be produced on the drainage of the Frodsham and Hedsby marshes should the powers sought be granted, and to take such steps as might be deemed necessary to protect the interests of the owners. The chair was oc- cupied by Mr. St. John Chorlton (agent to Lord Chol- mondeley), and there were present Messrs. C. E. Lina- ker, J. Brandreth, R. Bate, H. Tyley. and Thos. Ellames representing the f-llowing owners Mr. A. H. Talbot, Captain Higson, Wright Trustees. Hayes Trustees, New- ton Trustees, Mr. Ashley Harvey, FT. Linaker Trustees, and J. C. Lowe.—Mr. Linaker having explained the object of the meeting said there were 2,500 acres belong- ing to the landowners in the district of the Drainage Board, which drained into the waterway. In 1886, when the original Bill was promoted they got a clause inserted to the effect that if the water of the Canal and the river Weaver was backed up higher than the contrail of their sewers, and any damages occurred, the Canal. Company would make them good. So far they had not seen any material change made in the drainage. But the powers new seught were such that it was quite possible ' the water in the Canal might be raised to a higher perma- nent level. Even if that did not occur it might be raised between the throw of the tide and its return and held up for a longer period, thereby flooding the Frodsham Marshes. He had examined the deposited plans, and they evidently showed that the company were going to build the tidal openings about four feet, 8o that if they did not turn out the water through the sluices or lock gates speedily the water would be held up to the extent of four feet,whereby the drainage of Frodsham Marshes would be blocked up, and there would not be an acre of land not swamped.—The Chairman said it seemed to him very desirable that the landowners saoutd act in unison. It would be very easy to levy a scale of the cost by the acreage of the land belonging to each. Lord Cholmondelev owned something like 1,200 acres, and therefore the chief onus lay upon him.—Mr. Linaker in reply to Mr. Bate said abont 60 landowners were interested.—Mr. Brandreth informed the meeting what action had been taken by the Runcorn Rural District Council in the matter. He stated that if the level of the water were raised six feet it wonld almost reach Flelshv.--The Chairman having explained that the owners of 1,700 acres out of 2,300 acres were represented, it was decided after a little discussion, to engage an engineer to report on the subject. It was also decided to lodge a petition against the Bill on behalf of Lord Cholmondeley and other landowners on Frodsham and Helsby Marshes.—The meeting then terminated.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

DRUNKENNESS.

At the Police Station, Earlestown, on Monday morning, before Mr. C, B. F. Borron, James Cowley, of Liverpool, was charged with being drunk on Saturday night. In default of paying a fine of 2s. 6d. and costs, he was sent to goal for seven days!

LECTURE ON POULTRY KEEPING.

As will be seen from an advertisement, in con- nection with the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban Dis- trict Council Technical Instruction Committee, two lectures on poultry keeping will be delivered by Mr. Thomas Carr, lecturer on poultry keeping for the Lancashire County Council. The first will be given on Wednesday, February 19th in the Town Hall, Newton-le-Willows, and the second on Thurs- day February 20th,in the Co-Operative Hall, Earles- town. Councillor Wood will preside at the first lecture, and Councillor Emmett at the.second. All interested in the question of the proper rearing of poultry should attend. E A.RI,ESTOWN CRICKET CLUB.

A ball on a laig-e scale in aid of the funds of the above club was held in the Viaduct works on Mon- day evening. The room in which the soiree was held on the previous Friday night was placed at the disposal of the club committee, and dancing commenced about nine o'clock. There were about 700 people present, and dancing was kept up until about four o'clock next morning, to the strains of the Viaduct brass band. The arduous duties of M.C.'s were carried out efficiently by Mr. G. Shinn and Mr. James Barr.

_ On Saturday night a lecture was given in the St. John's Parochial Hall, Earlestown, by Mr. A. H. L. Evans, of Haydock,on "The Oberammergaus and their Passion Play." The room was crowded, the proceeds, which consisted of a silver collection, being divided between the St. John's Church exten- sion fund and the Haydock Curate's fund. The lecture was illustrated by 180 lime light views, and rrevious to entering upon the subject, pictures illustrating the " new photography" were exhibited, showing the bones in a human hand, the contents of a pocket and purse, &c. Mr. Evans handled his subject very eloquently, and played on the harmonium selections of the Passion music. Suitable hymns connected with the illustration were given by the S. John's choir.

CHILDREN'S OPER.ETTA AT

EARLESTOWN.

At the Town Hall, Earlestovra, before a large audi- ence, the senior scholars of the Earlestown District School gave a representation of the operetta, "The pay of the Pied Piper ; or the troubles of a District Council." These juvenile representations create the liveliest in- terest amongst the children and their parents, and whilst the youngsters derive some educational benefit from the instruction they receive in the preparation, they assist to a large extent the general cause of educa- tion. The entertainment on Wednesday night was for a two-fold object, that of enlarging the school library and providing prizes for the children attending the schools, the balance of the proceeds, after deducting ex- penses for the provision of a treat to be given to the children taking part in the entertainment, being devoted to the general fued.The children who were assembled on the platform to the number of about 150, presented a very pretty picture, the rich and varied dresses of the boys, mingled with the white, blue and pink dress of the girls rendering the scene very bright and pleasing. Briefly the plot or argument of the operetta winds about the town of Hamelin, which is infested with rats. The Council meet and consult as to the means to be adopted for ridding the place of the rodents. Terrified ladies rush into the Council Chamber and insist upon being present at that and all subsequent discussions. As a result of their joint deliberations the Council decide to advertise. The "Pied Piper" answers the advertisement, and is en- gaged by the council, after giving specimens of the power of his music by the aid of which he undertakes to deliver the town from the scourge of the rats. His music 'charms" the rats away to the river, where they are drowned. When the rats are gone the council refuse to pay the piper. Consequent upon this refusal the piper exercises the fascinating powers of his music on the children whom he threatens to-drown unless he is paid for his services. The council decide to pay,and all ends well. The principal characters in the operetta were : "Piper," Master Charles Rawling ; " Burgomaster," Master John Latham ; "Beadle," Master Richard Jones; Councillors, Herr Seidlitz (Master F. Banton), Herr Long (Tom Stringer), Herr Kirschwasser (Willie Drummond), Herr Sankrant (Joseph Jones). Herr Blumenlied (Alfred Cronin), Herr Schubert (F. Valentine), with Madera Morris, Halton, GreeneTackson, Macklin, Smith, Wilson, Marsden, and Gee ; councillors' wives and sisters. Frau Rubenstein (Miss C. Pye), Frau Long (Miss C.Ratcliffe), and Misses A. Green, P. Smith, F. Sproston,L.Mottram, E. Austen, A. Jones, J. Boughey, E. Eaton, M. Siddelev, E. Gee, E. Watkinsoa, F. Austin, M. Hazeldene, E. Hughes, and E. Hassan. The rats were represented by Masters F. Cooper, W. Deluce, W. Cooper, E. Spencer,l.T. Ogden. A. Rushton,' C. Marriott,: H. Lowe, J. Thomp- son, W. Alldricige, H. Mcllveen, A. Sutton, J. Clary. W. Clough, E. Tomlinson, T. Payne, W. Chadwick and W. Brown. The lady teachers of the school have spared neither time nor labour in the get up of the children who took a leading part in the operetta, the staff of the Infant Sehool also lending a helping hand. Miss Haworth and Miss Gilmour, assisted by Miss Cronin and Miss Pye, had charge of the dresses of the girls who took the part of councillors, wives, Sze. The boy's .Cos- tumes designed by Mr. George Sutton (Grant-st.), had been in the hands of Miss Nash, Miss A. Mo:yneux, and Miss Peet, assisted ley Miss Tripp and Miss Sutton. Mr. Brown, the head master, who of course had his lion's share of the work,had been ably assisted in the prepara- tion of the operetta by Mr. Stringer, Miss Nash and Miss A. Molyneux, great praise being due to them for:the way in which they had devoted themselves to the work. The orchestra was composed-of piano (Miss Annie Moly- neux), Ist violins (Miss Tripp, Miss Alice Trion. and Miss Adams), 2nd violins (Miss Nellie Tripp, and Master W. fittfiel(l). 'cello (Mr. H. Latimer). With the performance of the children we have no fault to find, as without exception all did exceptionally well. The opening chorus was given with zest, and was a forecast of the remaining musical items. The song of the Bergomaster (John Latham) was very nicely given, and the chorus was speedily taken up, the voices were well blended, and excellent time was maintained. The Beadle acted his part to perfection, and his singing left nothing to be desired. The best effort was perhaps that of Councillor Schubert (F. Valentine),who deserves every praise. He is the possessor of a clear voice of good range, and on each occasion on which he appeared in sone, he was heartily applauded. The dialogue was well carried out, the children performing their parts with perfect nonchalance. Of the composition of the dialogue at any rate these portions which contained local allu- sions. we cannot speak in very high terms of praise. It will he understood that the dialogue arises in the Coun- cil Chamber of the town of Hamelin, and it needed no very great stretch of imagination to see at once that many of the remarks were so directed as to refer, in- directly to members of the Newton Council. Again the allusions to the wares to be obtained at the establish- ments of local tradesmen, were not exactly in good taste, and the expressions fell very flat on the audience. In any future representation of the kind,it would be well it Mr. Brown would eliminate allusions of this kind. With this, of course, the children have nothing to do, and we cannot speak too highly of their pertormance. At the conclusion, Dr. Handyside moved a vote of thanks to the headmaster, his staff and his children for the performance they had given that nieht. They would understand the labour and pains involved to give such a performance. They were all aware that their annual examination had been pronounced excellent. It had always been an endeavour on the part of the managers of the school, to make the school second to none„and he thought they had attained to that position. Mr. A.Peake seconded, and accorded a tribute to the parents for their assistance towards the success of the performance, in getting the children ready. (Applause.) Mr. Brown, headmaster. was grateful far the public acknowledgment of . the efforts which one and all had made in securing the great success, and also to the parents for the great pains they took in seconding their efferts. (Applauee.) The entertainment was repeated on Thursday even- ing.

VIADUCT INSTITUTE, EARLESTOW N.

GRAND SOIREE. The distribution of prizes andi certificates gained during the sessions of 1894 and '95, by students attend- ing the Viaduct Institute Technical Instruction Classes were distributed on Friday evening by Mr. W. E. Dor- rington, of Manchester. a director of the London and North-VVestern Railway Company. The proceedings, which included a tea party, concert and ball, were, as on previous occasions, held in the Lifting Shop in the Viaduct works, a capacious building, most suitable for such an affair. For the past fort- night a large aumber of workmen have been busily

employed preparing the room, and under the guiding care of Mr. Harwood and Mr. C. W. Emmett, it speedily underwent a pleasing change and on Wednesday evening it was thrown open for public inspection,and there were many expressions of admiration. The decorations were carried out on something approaching the same lines as in former years, and the mottoes were equally as appro- priate. Tea was provided on Friday evening, when there were about 1,400 people present. After tea the company adjourned to the other half of the room, where the concert was held and the prizes distributed. Mr. J. W. Emmett presided, and he was supported by Mr. W. E. Dorrington, Mr. F. J. Wood, Mr. J. Vicars, Mr. W. Thompson, Mr. W. Collingwood, Mr. C. W. Emmett, Dr. Handyside, Revs. J. Bartley, E. R. Davies, F. E. Miller and E. W. W. 'Kaye and a number of ladies. After a quartette "Strange adventure," was cleverly rendered by Miss Smith, Miss Burtenshaw, Mr, Lawley and Mr. S. Cookson, The Chairman called upon the Secretary, Mr. T. Ormand, to raid his report of the classes. Mr. Ormand then submitted the following report : _ _ _ _ _

"Viaduct Institute, Barlestown. February 7th. 1896.—Mr. Chairnian. Ladies and Gentlemen,—ln consideration of the very heavy duties to be performed by Mr. Dorrington in dis- tributing the prizes and certificates to no less than 190 Students, we feel sure no apology is required for reducing our report to the smallest possible limits, .consistent with a brief review of the salient features of the work accomplished in the past two years. • Number of classes :—During the session 1893-4 classes were conducted at Earlestown in 18 subjects. In 1894 and 1895 branch classes were established at Newton and the following subjects taught. viz., Bookkeeping, Shorthand, Cookery, Laundry, and Dressmaking which together with the 18 sub- jects taken up at the Institute, give a total of 23 subjects for the Beast n just closed. _ _

Newton Claises :—You will readily understand .that the

inauguration of classes at the Newtint end of the township considerably increased our lxpenses and was one of the chief factors in augmenting our already adverse-balance. We regret to report that the, response at Newton for the current session has not been encouraging, the entries being too few to justify the Committee in conducting classes in more than two subjects—Laundry and Shorthand. Wargrave Classes :—To, meet the requirements of a numer- ous population at Wargrave, tha Committee have arranged for a course of 24 lectures to be given this winter on Cookery. which we believe are being highly appreciated, and we hope may lead to a still further development of Technical Instruc- tion in that part o! the district. (county Council Grant :—Owing to a reduction in the County Council grant the Committe 3 have very reluctantly been compelled to discontinue the classes in Needlework, Sick Nursing. and Intermediate and advanced Book-keeping, as the number of entries for these subjects did not warrant the expenditure their continuance would have entailed. Handwriting and Commercial Corresponden:e :—But whilst relinquishing classes for which there was a limited demand, and which had in some measure to meet the redu?tion of grant from the County Council. we have instituted classes in Hand- writing and commercial correspondence at Earlestown and Newton, which have met with very great success, and supply a IPng experienced want. The number of Students enrolled at Earlestown is 54 and at. Newton 38.

Poultry Ke.ping : While dealing with the new subjects we would here state that the County Council Agricultural Committee have sanctioned two lectuies being given on Poul- try Keeping, one at Newton and one at Earlestown. The first lecture will be delivered at the Town Hall, New- ton, on the 19th February, and the secand one at the Co- operative Hall, Earlestown, on Thursday, the 20th February, and we sincerely trust every person interested in the question of "fowls and hsw to make them pay," will endeavour to secure for the Lecturer an appreciative audience. Statistics :—To obviate the necessity of including in this report the statistics relating to the classes, the committee have issued in pamphlet form, a copy of which we trust has been given to each person, such information as we hope will prove of service to everyone desirous of seeinß a still greater progress in Technical Education, particularly in this district. Art :—ln 1895, 100 students were enrolled, against 52 in 1894. Althoucds this is a large increase, it is to be regretted that so few of the older Science students joined the Art classes, knowing as they do the important part, Model and Perspective drawing playa in forming the basis of nearly all Technical I,•struction. and the facility for making Free-hand sketches being of untold advantage, not only in the exam- inations but in the workshop. Science :—lt is not our intention to consider separately each Science subject, but we cannot refrain from expressing surprise at the slow growth of the classes in Magnetism and Electricity. It is a subject which appeals to a great body of intelligent artisans, more particularly so when we consider the vast development in electricity in recent years. and the still greater development which is expected in this science in the near future. Gold Medal :—At the distribution of prizes in May, 1894, Dr. Handyside very generously promised a gold medal to be awarded to the most suszessful student_for the year 1895.

This distinction has been won by Harry Eddington,employed by the Vulcin Foundry, Limited, His successes are as fol- lows :—E. pass, practical plane and solid geometry; Ist A., machine clnstruction ;B. pass, applied mechanics. . .

Although the examinatiOn results are in many respects of a gratifying character, yet they fall quite below what might reasonably be expected if we had a properly organised system of education, which would bridge the gulf which exists in this town between the public elementary schools and the Seence and Art Classes. To insure the success of Technical Instruction adequate provision ought to be made for continu- ing the education of the boys and girls on leaving the Public Elementary schools. If this were done, we should have a constant and steady supply of pupils, not only anxious but suitable for higher education which could be adapted to the varied walks of life they are likely to follow, and which edu- cation at the present time is seriously retarded by their very imperfect knowledge of its elementary principles. May we venture to hope that continuation schools, the importance of which is known to every educationalist, will be an accom- plished fact b.fore the end of the current year. The Institute Committee of Management are hopin they will. be succ.ssful _

. In their applicationfibis year for a course of Gilchrist lectures, which, if granted will, we have every reason to believe, stimulate and materially assist in furthering such an object as the establishment of continuation evening schools. If the decision of the trustees is favourable, the co-operation of every Interest in the town likely to be contributory to the success of such lectures will be solicited. _ _

To those who have not been so successful in the recent ex- aminations as they desire, we would ask them to remember that the failure that teaches us that our knowledge at Its beat

is meagre,- that gathers and concentrates our wandering powers, and nerves us into truer and enlarged views of life an.l duty is a fortunate failure." The Chairman thought they would all agree with him when be said that the report which they had just heard read was a very good one. Of course, the report could have been made a great deal longer, had the Secretary gone into all the matters which he might have done,but in order to curtail that report as much as possible, he had issued a pamphlet which gave all information with regard to the prize fund and the balance sheet as well. He (the Chairman) did not intend to make any speech, they had such a very long programme to go through, and he did not think they wanted to hear him. There was one little matter which he wished to bring before the students that evening, and that was Sir Richard Moon's scholarship. He might tell them that Sir Richard Moon a few months ago wrote him, and asked him how it was that none of the Earlestown students had gained his scholarship. The scholarship was worth £BO a year for two years. He had a great difficulty in answering thse letter; he hardly knew what to say, but he came to the conclusion that it was on account of the students not continuing ta attend the classes sufficiently long to gain the knowledge requires of them to compete for these schole.rohips. He sincerely hoped that the students would do all they could to secure the scholarships. When Sir R. Moon was Chairman of the Company he took a great interest in the Wagon Department, and he might say here that the wagons were classed third—the en- gines first, carriagea second, wagons last. He wanted them to put the wagons first, and to beat the loco and carriage as well,and he hoped they would do that in the coming session. (Hear. hear.) Another item he wished to name: he did not know whether all the students knew it or not, but he would tell them. The committee were very generous to the students ; if there were any other classes held which were not held in connection with the Viaduct Institute and the students wantel to go to Liverpool or Manchester, they were allowed by the committee half their railway fare. (Applause.) He had now a very pleasing duty to discharge, and he was sure he should be speaking the sentiments of that meeting when he said that they had been favoured with the pre- sence of Mr. Dorrington, one of their directors, who had come there to distribute the priz-s at great inconvenience to himself, and he was sure they would accord him a very hearty reception. (Applause.) Mr. W. H. Dorrington expressed the great satisfaction it gave him to be present at the proceedings that even- ing. He had that !feeling of !satisfaction in a double capacity, first of all as a director of the London and North-Western Railway Co., again as a Lancashire man, born and bred, because he liked to come amongst Lancashire faces. (Applause). It was his privilege to offer, on behalf of himself and those on the platform, congratulations to those who had been successful in winning the prizes which he was going to distribute,and he would single out for special congratulation, the name of Mr. Harry Edington, who had won the gold medal. •given by Dr. Handyside. That gentleman wished him to state how sorry he was he could not stay to witness the presentation of that meth]. To all the prize win- ners he would offer his hearty congratulations and he trusted their winnings this year might only be an ear- nest of what. was to come in the future. But he should like to offer a word of consolation to those who had striven for the pr;zes, but had not been successful. Most men learned more by their failures than by their suc- cesses, and if those who had failed in endeavours this year would only take it the right way, and nerve them- selves to fresh efforts, they could wipe out the memory of present failure by the success of the future. (Hear, hear.) The great necessity there was for scientific and technical education in this country was shown by the keen competition going on with other countries. There was a time when Great Britain could afford to fold her arms, and let technical education slide, be- cause she was so much superior to other nations in education, but .there was not then the competition that there is to-day. Now they saw foreign nations taking a leaf out of our book ; they had gone ahead, and nerved themselves for a struggle; they I were educating, and if England's sons were to compete

against those sent out from other nations they must be I educated, or they would be left behind in the race. As an instance of what a nation could do, he would refer them to a country which had been much talked about lately ; Japan. Less than 30 years ago Japan was shut out from all outside influence. She, however, opened her doors and educated her people and she was now the rival of Great Britain in the industrial enterprises of the world. He was curious to see how education went on in Japan, and whether it was made a national effort. School attendance in Japan was obligatcry, and she rocognised the necessity of education in her girls schools. There were ever 30,000 schools of various creeds,besides military and scientific schools. The last returns showed 82,000 teachers, anct 2,800,000 pupils, between the ages of six and 14. There were one or two things he should like to refer to in the report they had just heard. Their Secretary told them there were to be lectures upon the keeping of poultry, and how so make it pay. When he saw that in the report, he thought he would see what was the value of eggs, imported into this country from abroad. For the year ended December 31st last, he found there had been imported into Great Britain, eggs to the value of £4,000,000. In the two preceding years, it was very little less than that. He thought there must be something wrong with an industry which might be very well worked at home, when we had to pay out so much money for eggs from abroad. Some people had protested that the way was to giver up cocks and hens, and produce artificial eggs, but he hoped when they had to crack their artificial eggs in the morning for breakfast they would not find those ominous words " made in Germany " seamped on the inside. (Laughter.) Another subject was that of electricity. He was surprised the classes in electricity bad not been very well taken up. He should have thought that electricity would have appealed to every man of a scientific turn of mind. Electricity had annihilated time by giv- ing us the telegraph,it had extended the area of speech by giving us the telephone, it had given us the electric light. Electricity seemed to have become a commercial success for motive power on land, because we had elec- tric railways and electric trams, and before long we should find someone getting hold of it for propulsion by sea, and he did, not think the time was far distant when we should have aluminium ships propelled by electri- city. Electricity had given us also the new photography. He would like to say that in the coming session,all those who were studying would study in a spirit of confidence and determination to get all out of their studies not merely for the prizes themselves, but for the power it gave. (Applause.) ,The distribution of prizes was then proceeded with, about 190 students receiving awards ranging in value from about three shillings to nearly £5. At the conclusion of the distribution, Mr. Dorrinetott said he had conferred with Mr. Emmett and had ob- tained his permission to offer a gold medal for the most successful student during the present session,w leo should have attained the standard of excellence the committee in their judgment so decide upon. (Applause.) He had also at the same time to thank Dr. Handyside,who gave a medal last year, for his repeated offer, nut be would ask him to let it stand over for one year, and allow him (Mr. Dorrington) to come in between,because he wished to mark his appreciation of the work of the Institute, and his great pleasure in coming there that night. (Ap I plause.) _ _ _ _ _

- Mr. C. W. Emmett moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Dorrington. They had all been instructed by the clever address given by Mr. Dorrington, and they were honoured by his presence. At times they. were apt to think that their directors did not take that interest in their social welfare and matters of that kind as they thought they ought to do. Mr. Dorrington however took a great interest not only in their lastitute, but also in those at Wolverton and Crewe. The Rev. F. E. Miller seconded. .

Mr. Dorrington in acknowledgment, thought he ought rather to return thanks to them for the exceedingly cor- dial welcome on this his first, but he hoped, not his last visit to Earlestown. He should like to assure them on behalf of his colleagues on the Board of the Company that they took the deepest interest in all those who worko for the Company in every branch. It was a very great area that the Company covered, and it would be almost impossible for the Directors to go everywhere, but they came amongst their employes whenever they could do so. (Applause.) _ _

Mr. F. J. Wood, proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Em- mett for presiding. Mr.' W. Thompson seconded. Mr. Emmett said he was obliged for the kind expres- sions. During the 28 years the had been at Earles- town, he had tried his best to encourage the young men to fit themselves for something better than working at the bench, and he hoped the prizes offered by Mr. Dor- rington and Dr. Handyside would stimulate them to do their best, and that they would take Sir Richard Moon's scholarship. He was anxious they should take that, as they did not want to be last in the race. (Applause.) During the evening an excellent programme of songs was submitted by a talented company of singers, includ- ing Miss Marie Smith (soprano) Miss Alice Bertenshaw (contralto) Mr. Walter Lawley (tenor) Mr. Stanley Cook- son (bass) and Mr. Lester Barrett,whilst Mr. W. Pickard efficiently carried out the onerous task of accompanist. Mr. Stanley Cookson, was enthusiastically received, and his efforts included "Star of the Desert," "The Band- olero," and "Eileen Alannnah," each of which was given in a rich and finished style, !and earned spontan- eous applause. Miss Marie Smith, at once established a good reputation, her best selection perhaps being," Poor Wandering one." her other items being " Seenps that are brightest," and "Killarney. Mr. Walter Lawley's rendering of "Lend me your aid," was cleverly wrought, the lengthy recitative being given in a mariner which left nothing to be desired. He was also successful in gaining the vlaudits of the audience by his singing of " Tom Bowling." Miss Alice Bertenshavr,a powerful contralto, gave excellent intrepretations of the songs," Grannie's Story," and " Carita," and her efforts were duly appre- ciated. The duet, " When the wind bloweth in by Miss Maria Smith and Mr. Cookson was well rendered, as also was the quartette," A regular royal queen." Mr. Lester Barrett received a hearty welcome and his selec- tions created the greatest amusement, each item being of course encored. His contributions included " Christ- mas time, " " By the sad sea waves," "Spring cleaning," ** All fine girls, &c. At the conclusion of the concert the room was cleared, and dancing commenced, to the music of the Viaduct brass band, and it was kept up with spirit until about five o'clock next morning. The M.C.'s were Mr. G. Shinn and Mr. J. Davies. The festivities were continued cn Saturday.evening, when another dance was held until nearly midnight, there being about 500 people present.

DESERTION OF A-BOY AT NEWTON

A FATHER SENT TO PRISON.

Thomas Hitchin, Mercer-at., Newton, was charged at the Newton Petty Sessiens on Saturday, with neglecting his son, 13 years, on divers dates. Mr. J. W. Knowles. solicitor, who appeared on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., said that five or six years ago, the boy in question had been living with an aunt at Wigan,but she could not afford to keep him any longer, and she brought him to Newton to his father, the defendant. The defendant kept him a few days, and then took him out into the street and told him to go either to his aunt's in Wigan, or zo zo the Workhouse. The boy was then only six or seven years of age. He was taken in by some persons named Single- ton, poor people, and remained with them until the present time. He slept there so far as could be gathered, and got most of his food where he could, but not from his father. His father had given him nothing. Sinele- ton'a, no doubt, did what they could for him. Since January Ist. Mrs. Ward, fish dealer, Newton, feeling I sorry for him, allowed the lad to run errands for her.and for that she had given him his dinner and tea. At the time Mrs. Ward first saw the boy.he was almost starved to death. The lad's face would even yet speak for it- ear, and unless there was a radical change in his condi- tion, he would not be in this world very long. It was quite time the case was taken up, and he asked the magistrates to give the father a very severe penalty for neglecting his child for so many years. The father was getting regularly 17s. a week, but he was too fend of his drink. Inspector McAteer, N.S.P.C.C., said that he visited the boy on Jan. 4, in company with P.C. Williamson.at the house of Mrs. Ward, 63, High-st., Newton. at which place he had been frr some days prJviously. He was then insufficiently clad and appeared to be suffering from consumption. and had a very bad cough. Witness had since seen the father at his lodgings and the latter told him that he could do nothing for him. He was earning 17s. a week constantly, and paid 10s. a week for his board and lodging. Witness asked him what he did with the remainder, but he could not tell. He ad- mitted that he had done nothing for the boy, but said that the boy was able to work for himself. The boy was then called, and said that his father had not fOund either food or clothes for the last five or six rears.

Mrs. Ward said that the boy came to her shop three weeks the previous Monday. Be looked very ill,and she took him in. He told her afterwards that his father bad left him in the street and said that he could gb to the Workhouse. Since the time he had mile to her house, she had kept him with the exception of his breakfast. He ,slept at the house of Mrs. Singleton. Be rAn little errands for witness. P.C. Williamson said that the defendant had been in regular work since Optober. Be was very much ad- dicted to drink. • Emily Singleton was then called by the defendant and said that the boy had been at her house for four years but his father had not paid her anything during that time. She was only a poor woman and could not afford to do much for him. Defendant liked his drink. The defendant said that he was out of work for a long time, and also asserted that he gave what money he could to Mrs. Singleton. The magistrates retired and when they returned, Mr. Gillespie, who presided, said that that was the most heartless case that had been brought before the court for a long time. The defendant did not seem to care what became of the boy. He would be sent to gaol for one month's imprisonment._

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that the Right Honor- able Sir Matthew White Ridley, Baronet, Her Partm'aenPtr,inci beajesty for the Home Principal Secretary cf State has in pursuance of the powers conferred 4:thim by the above Acts, and upon the application of e County Council of this County, made an Order ap- ITheable to the whole of the County of Chester, provid- Ing (inter alia) as follows: 1. The time during which the killing and taking of wild birds or any of them is prohibited by the Act of 1880 shall bt- extended in the County of Chester so as to be from the Ist day of March to the 12th day of August in each year. 2. The Act of 1880 as extended by Section 1 of this Order shall apply within the whole of the County of Chester to the Kestrel and Heron as if those species were included in the Schedule of that Act. 3. The taking or destroying of the eggs of the Owl, the Bittern, the Kingfisher, the Kestrel, the Heron. the Shelldrake, the Dnolin, the Black- headed Gull, the Common Tern, and the Oyster Catcher shall be prohibited in the whole of the County of Chester. " Ha 1 is it possible ? " asked the minister, with s look of surprise and interest. "Aye, was II And you shall hear all about it!'' said Old Hurricane. And upon that he commenced and told the minister the adventure of Capitol° with Black Donald at Hurricane Hall. The minister was amazed, yet could not forbear to say: "It seems to me, however, that it was Capitol; who was within a hair's breadth of capturing thhi notorious desperado!" " Pooh I she clung to him like the reckless lunati! that she is; but lord, he would have carried her oa on his back if it had not been for ME." The minister smiled a little to himself and the!, said: "This protegee of yours is a very remarkable girl; as interesting to me in her character, as she Is in het history; her very spirit, courage and insubordina- tion make her singularly hard to manage and apt tO go astray. With your permission I will make her acquaintance, with the view of seeing what good can do her." "Pray, do so, for then you will be better able tO counsel me how to manage the capricious little witch, who if I attempt to check her in her wild and dangerous freedom of action, tells me plainll that liberty is too precious a thing to be exchanged for food and clothing, and that rather than live in bondage she would throw herself upon the protec- tion of the court I—if she does that the game is up' Le Noir, against whom we can as yet prove nothing. would claim her as his niece and ward, and get het Into his power for the purpose of making away with her, as he did with her father and mother." "Oh! for heaven's sake, sir, no more of that until we have further evidence," said the minister, uneasily, adding—" I will see your very interesting protegee to-morrow." "Do I do I to-morrow, to-day, this hour, anytimer said Major Warfieid, as he cordially took leave of the pastor. (To be continue'.)

BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMEN. The information contained in this book ought to be known by every married woman, and it will not harm the unmarried to read. The book is clnveniently divided into 12 chapters. The first chapter treats of the changes at pnberty,or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint ; points out the best age for marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful in- formation that one can ordinarily get only from an in- telligent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage of blood relations; and condemns such marriages as a rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The fifth chapter tells how a woman should live during the pregnant state. The sixth chapter treats ot mishaps and how to avoid them. The seventh chapter treats cf maternal impressions and shows that birthmarks are not due tolongings on the part of the mother, but rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made comparatively easy if certain rules are obeyed ; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of the proper management of confinement until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat the mother till she is tip and about again. The 'eleventh oliapter treats of sterility ; gives the main causes of it, how these may he overcome and children result The last chapter treats of the change," a most important article for all women over forty. The book is full of useful information and no book is written which goes so thoroughly into matters relating to married women. Some may think too much is told ; such can scarcely be the case, for knowledge is power and the means of attaining hapniness. The book can be bad from Dr. T• R. ALLINSON, 25 Box, 4, t.panish-place Manchester-square, London, W., in return for a Postal crder for is. Li.
